Dirac Operators on Orbifold Resolutions: Uniform Elliptic Theory
V. F. Majewski

Abstract
. Dirac operators on Riemannian spaces play a central role in various branches of mathematics, encoding rich geometric and topological data. They appear as deformation operators in moduli problems, including those associated with special holonomy metrics, gauge theory instantons, and calibrated submanifolds. This paper investigates the behaviour of families of Dirac operators as the underlying Riemannian spaces degenerate to a Riemannian orbifold in the so called adiabatic limit. Specifically, we focus on relating the kernels and cokernels of the family of Dirac operators to adiabatic data and establish uniform bounds on their right inverse. These results provide a crucial analytic foundation for gluing problems on orbifold resolutions, without relying on the intricate iterated edge calculus developed by Mazzeo, Melrose, Schulze [ Maz91 , Mel92 , Mel96 , Sch91 ] and others. This work paves the way for forthcoming studies, where these techniques will be used to address open problems in special holonomy geometry, for example the construction of compact G 2 {\mathrm{G}}_{2} - and Spin ⁡ ( 7 ) {\mathrm{Spin}}(7) -manifolds, gauge theory, and calibrated geometry. The methods developed here have broader implications for the study of singular Riemannian spaces and their analytic properties.



1.
Preface

Dirac operators on Riemannian spaces are of great interest in many branches of mathematics such as differential and algebraic geometry, mathematical physics, and geometric analysis. These operators are square roots of Laplace operators and their analytic realisations encode rich geometric and topological data. In Riemannian geometry and gauge theory Dirac operators appear as deformation operators of various moduli problems such special holonomy metrics, holonomy instantons and calibrated submanifolds. Compactifications of subject moduli-spaces usually contain Riemannian orbifolds as boundary points and hence the following question naturally arises:

How does a family of Dirac operators behave as the underlying family of Riemannian spaces
1
1

1

Here we intentionally use the ambiguous class of Riemannian spaces which includes (singular) Riemannian manifolds as well as Riemannian orbifolds.
degenerate to a Riemannian orbifold?

This question arises naturally in many contexts, prominently in gluing problems, and is interesting in itself.

Instead of asking for a family of Riemannian spaces that degenerates to a Riemannian orbifold, we will fix a Riemannian orbifold and ask for a family of “less singular” Riemannian spaces that “converges” to subject Riemannian orbifold in the so called
adiabatic limit
. We will refer to such a family of Riemannian spaces as a Gromov-Hausdorff resolution.

Construction methods for moduli problems in differential geometry usually involve solving nonlinear elliptic differential equations. Solutions to which are often constructed via gluing methods. Hereby, local solutions to these nonlinear differential equations are interpolated to construct a so-called preglued solutions. These preglued solutions solve the nonlinear equations in an open region and only fail closely to be global solutions. Deforming the preglued solution by a small perturbation to an honest solutions amounts to solve a so-called gluing problem. The following examples will briefly explain how such gluing problems naturally arise in the construction of special holonomy metrics, the construction of special holonomy instantons and the construction of calibrated submanifolds, by resolving orbifold resolutions.

1.

Harmonic forms on a compact manifolds, can be identified with the kernel of the Hodge-de Rham operator and are in representatives of cohomology classes. The Hodge-de Rham operator, squares to the Laplacian on differential forms and is commonly known to be a Dirac operator associated to the Dirac bundle of differential forms. Using the Meyer-Vietoris sequence, we are able to compute the cohomology of an orbifold resolution with relative ease. In contrast to the simplicity of understanding the cohomology of the resolution, it is unclear however, how the harmonic representatives of the cohomology classes behave, as the Gromov-Hausdorff resolution approaches the orbifold limit.

In order to answer this question, we need to carefully study the kernel of the Dirac operators and relate their kernels to an approximate kernel arising from interpolating harmonic spinors on the orbifold and on the resolution data.

2.

Spaces with special holonomy metrics, such as Calabi-Yau-,
{\mathrm{G}}_{2}
- and
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-metrics, are of central interest in both mathematics and physics due to their unique geometric properties and their role in supersymmetric string theory compactifications. In particular, all special holonomy spaces are Ricci-flat. These metrics are often constructed by resolving singularities in orbifolds with special holonomy metrics. A famous example of which is the Kummer construction of
K3
surfaces by resolving the hyperkähler orbifold
\mathbb{T}^{4}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. In recent years, Joyce and Karigiannis
[
Joy96c
,
Joy96b
,
Joy96a
,
JK21
]
generalised these ideas to resolve certain (flat) orbifolds to construct compact
{\mathrm{G}}_{2}
and
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-manifolds.

Let
(X^{t},\Omega^{t},g^{t})
be an adiabatic Ricci-flat resolution of a special holonomy orbifold
(X,\Omega,g)
. Here
\Omega^{t}
and
\Omega
determine the special holonomy metric. All these existence results for torsion-free special holonomy structures can be formulated as solutions of a nonlinear differential equation of the form

\displaystyle-({\mathrm{d}}+{\mathrm{d}}^{*_{\Omega^{t}}})\eta=T(\Omega^{t})+Q_{\Omega^{t}}(\eta).

Here,
{\mathrm{d}}+{\mathrm{d}}^{*_{\Omega}}
denote the Hodge-de Rham operator with respect to
g_{\Omega^{t}}
,
T(\Omega^{t})
denote the torsion of
\Omega^{t}
and
Q_{\Omega^{t}}
denotes nonlinear tensorial map satisfying a quadratic estimate. The Hodge-de Rham operator
{\mathrm{d}}+{\mathrm{d}}^{*_{\Omega^{t}}}
is commonly known to be a Dirac operator. Its kernel consists of harmonic forms. On compact Riemannian manifolds, Hodge theory links these harmonic forms to cohomology classes and thus to topological data of the underlying manifold.

3.

Moduli spaces of instantons or monopoles are the fundamental objects of interest in gauge theories. Famously, ASD instantons on four-manifolds were used by Donaldson
[
Don83
]
to define enumerative invariants for smooth structures of four-manifolds. To ensure the existence of these moduli spaces, solutions to instanton or monopole equations are usually constructed by hand, e.g. the moduli space of
{\mathrm{PU}}(n)
-instantons on
K3
surfaces is constructed by resolving flat orbifold instantons. In higher dimensions, gauge theories on special holonomy spaces were famously studied by Donaldson and Thomas
[
DT98
]
. The instanton equations are given by

\displaystyle*(\Omega\wedge F_{A})=\lambda\cdot F_{A}\Leftrightarrow F_{A}^{\Omega}=0.

Starting from an orbifold
\Omega
-instanton
A
, we hope to resolve it by a smooth
\Omega^{t}
-instanton on the orbifold resolution. Given a pre-glued (adiabatic) solution
A^{t}
, a deformation
A^{t}+a
is an
\Omega^{t}
instanton if
a
satisfies the nonlinear differential equation

\displaystyle-D^{\Omega^{t}}_{A^{t}}a=F^{\Omega^{t}}_{A^{t}}+\frac{1}{2}[a\wedge a]^{\Omega^{t}}

where
D_{A^{t}}^{\Omega^{t}}
is the
A^{t}
twisted Dirac operator on
(X^{t},g^{t})
and
F^{\Omega^{t}}_{A^{t}}
the
\Omega^{t}
-holonomy component of the curvature of the preglued connection
A^{t}
.

4.

Let
(X,g)
be a Riemannian space whose Riemannian structure is induced by a calibration
\Omega
, i.e. closed differential form satisfying the comass condition

\displaystyle|\Omega|\leq 1.

Examples of such calibrations are Calabi-Yau-, hyperkähler-,
{\mathrm{G}}_{2}
- and
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-structures. A calibrated subspace of such a calibrated Riemannian spaces is given by an immersed subspace
\iota:[C\rightarrow X]
such that

\displaystyle\Omega|_{C}={\mathrm{vol}}_{g_{C}}.

In the context of the examples of calibrations, these calibrated submanifolds are holomorphic submanifolds and special Lagrangian-, as well as (co-)associative-, and Cayley-submanifolds.

Let
C^{t}
be an almost
\Omega^{t}
-calibrated submanifold and
n
a section of its normal bundle. The calibration
\Omega^{t}
embeds the normal bundle of
C^{t}
into a graded Hermitian Dirac bundle. The condition for finding a
\Omega^{t}
-calibrated submanifold close to
C^{t}
can be formulated as solutions of the nonlinear differential equation

\displaystyle-D_{C^{t}}n=e(C^{t})+q^{t}(n).

Here
D_{C^{t}}
denotes a Dirac operator on the normal bundle of the almost calibrated submanifold
C^{t}
.

Notice that all the mentioned gluing problems can be written as

(1)

\displaystyle-D^{t}\Phi=E^{t}+Q^{t}(\Phi)

for a family of Dirac operators
D^{t}
on an orbifold resolution
(X^{t},g^{t})
. In order to solve these gluing problems, it is essential to construct a right-inverses to subject Dirac operators, such that a solution to (
1
) is given by a solution to the fixed-point problem

(2)

\displaystyle\Phi=-E^{t}+Q^{t}(R_{D^{t}}\Phi).

In order to solve this fixed point problem via the contraction mapping principal, we need to construct and bound the family of right-inverses.

In forthcoming work by the author
[
Maj25c
,
Maj25b
,
Maj25a
]
, the existence of
G_{2}
- and
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-orbifold resolutions is proved,
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-instantons, i.e. absolute minimisers of the Yang-Mill energy on
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-manifolds, and Cayley submanifolds, i.e. submanifolds calibrated by
{\mathrm{Spin}}(7)
-structures, are constructed using the results presented in this paper.

In the following we will outline the general construction and its difficulties:

Riemannian orbifolds admit
straficiations
by conjugacy classes of their isotropy groups. Naturally a Riemannian orbifold
(X,g)
decomposes into its regular part
X^{reg}
and the singular part
X^{sing}
which is of codimension
\geq 2
. Normal neighbourhoods of its singular strata of isotropy
\Gamma
are diffeomorphic to fibre bundles of (singular) cones over
\mathbb{S}^{m-1}/\Gamma
. Riemannian orbifold structures are natural examples of so called iterated edge metrics, by means of expansions close to their singular strata whose “leading order” is given by a
conically fibred (CF)
Riemannian structure. Notice that this CF structure has the geometric structure of a wrapped product

\displaystyle(X_{0},g_{0})=(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times Y,{\mathrm{d}}r^{2}+g_{Y;r}).

Here
\vartheta:(Y,g^{2,0}_{Y}+r^{2}g_{Y}^{0,2})\rightarrow(S,g_{S})
is a compact, collapsing Riemannian fibration of totally geodesic (singular) homology spheres. Thus Riemannian orbifolds will be though of as
conically singular fibred (CSF)
spaces, which will be defined in the scope of this paper.

We will distinguish three classes of Riemannian orbifolds by the complexity of their singular strata, which is related to the depth of the latter. The mildest class of singular strata will be referred to as type (I) singularities. Their local model is given by
\mathbb{R}^{n}/\Gamma=\mathbb{R}^{s}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}/\Gamma
, where
\Gamma={\mathrm{Isot}}(S)
is acting freely on
\mathbb{R}^{m}\backslash\{0\}
and consequently their normal cone bundle is a nonsingular conical fibration (CF) over the singular stratum. The total space of the normal cone bundle of a singular strata of type (I) is a noncompact Riemannian orbifold whose singular stratum can be identified with the zero section. Throughout this paper, we will mainly focus on orbifolds with one singular stratum of type (I) in order to avoid the notational overload by the bookkeeping of the required data. The general case can be derived from the subject set-up.

Given a connected singular stratum and a tubular neighbourhood
{\mathrm{Tub}}_{3\epsilon}(S)\subset X
, we can expand a Hermitian Dirac bundle structure
(E,{\mathrm{cl}}_{g},h,\nabla^{h})
on
(X,g)
with respect to the Riemannian distance to the singular stratum
S
and identify the zeroth order term as a (singular) conically fibred (CF) Hermitian Dirac bundle structure on the normal cone bundle
\nu_{0}:X_{0}=NS/{\mathrm{Isot}}(S)\rightarrow S
of
S
.

A
Gromov-Hausdorff resolution of a Riemannian orbifold

(X,g)
is given by a smooth family of (singular) Riemannian orbifolds
(X^{t},g^{t})
and morphisms

\displaystyle\rho^{t}:X^{t}\dashrightarrow X

such that
\rho^{t}
restricts to an orbifold diffeomorphism
(\rho^{t})^{-1}(X^{reg})\cong X^{reg}
, the exceptional set
\Upsilon^{t}=(\rho^{t})^{-1}(X^{sing})
is of codimension
\geq 2
and

\displaystyle(X^{t},g^{t})\xrightarrow[GH]{t\to 0}(X,g)\hskip 28.45274pt\text{and}\hskip 28.45274pt(X^{t}\backslash\Upsilon^{t},g^{t})\xrightarrow[C^{\infty}]{t\to 0}(X^{reg},g)

In particular
{\mathrm{vol}}_{g^{t}}(\Upsilon^{t})\xrightarrow{t\to 0}0
and
\rho^{t}_{*}g^{t}\xrightarrow[C^{\infty}]{t\to 0}g
.

Such resolutions are usually constructed via resolutions of the CF-Hermitian Dirac bundle over the normal cone bundle consist of a family of
asymptotically conically fibred (ACF)
Hermitian Dirac bundle-structures on a Riemannian fibration subject to a list of assumptions. The resolutions
(X^{t},g^{t})
and the resolutions of the Hermitian Dirac bundles are then constructed by interpolating between the ACF structures on the resolutions
(X_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta},g_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta})
of the normal cone
(X_{0},g_{0})
and the Hermitian orbifold Dirac bundle structures on
X
along a (shrinking) tubular neighbourhood of
S
.

In order to analyse the resulting family of Dirac operators
D^{t}
on the resolved orbifolds
(X^{t},g^{t})
we split the latter into three topological regions: the ACF part, the CF-part and the CSF-part. We will proceed by analysing all the different components individually in order to derive a uniform elliptic theory on the total space of the resolution.

We begin by considering the CF-part of the orbifold resolution. This part more generally referred to as the neck part of the gluing construction, can be realised as an annulus subbundle of the normal cone bundle of shrinking outer diameter. In this region, the
analytic behaviour of
\textbf{D}^{\textbf{t}}
is determined by the normal operator
\widehat{\textbf{D}}_{\textbf{0}}
of
D
, which can be seen as the leading order operator in an asymptotically expansion of
D
close to the singular stratum. By using essentially an elaborated version of separation of variables we can explicitly expand the kernel of
\widehat{D}_{0}
. Using this expansion, we argue that
\widehat{D}_{0}
,
D
and
\widehat{D}_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}
, the latter being a resolution of
\widehat{D}_{0}
, should be realised as bounded operators on
weighted function spaces
, subject to
boundary conditions
. By carefully choosing the right function spaces the normal operator can be realised as an isomorphism of Banach spaces;
\widehat{D}_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}
and
D
as Fredholm maps.

We continue by defining the so called approximate (co)kernel of
D^{t}
by interpolating the (co)kernel of the operators
\widehat{D}_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}
and
D
. One of the scopes of this paper is to prove the exactness of linear gluing, by virtue of the exactness of the sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{ker}}(D^{t})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{xker}}(D^{t})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{xcoker}}(D^{t})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{coker}}(D^{t}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{t}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{ob}}^{t}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{t}}

In order to prove the above, we will need to study the so called (anti-) gluing equations in the style of Hutchings-Taubes obstruction bundle gluing
[
HT07
,
HT09
]
.

Further, we will prove the existence of right-inverses to the operator
D^{t}
and construct adapted norms, for which these right-inverse are uniformally bounded. This problem arises due to the
collapsing phenomena
in the ACF-part of the linear gluing, causing the following problem.

Notice that the CF-Hermitian Dirac bundle on the normal cone bundle
\nu_{0}:X_{0}\rightarrow S
is a
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
-module, lifting the action of
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
on the normal cone bundle
X_{0}
by scaling the fibres. We will assume that this
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
-action can be lifted to the ACF Hermitian Dirac bundle structures
(X_{t^{2}\zeta},g_{t^{2}\zeta})
by means of the existence of a family of ACF structures
(X_{\zeta},g^{t}_{\zeta})
resolving the dilated normal cone bundle
(X_{0},g^{t}_{0})
.
“Pulling back along this action”, the associated Dirac operator
\widehat{D}^{t}_{\zeta}
has the form

\displaystyle\widehat{D}^{t}_{\zeta}=\widehat{D}^{t}_{\zeta;H}+t^{-1}\cdot D_{\zeta;V}

where
\widehat{D}^{t}_{\zeta;H}=\mathcal{O}(1)
in
t\to 0
. The vertical Dirac operator
D_{\zeta;V}
scales by
t^{-1}
and hence becomes singular in the limit
t\to 0
. In particular, the norm of sections that are vertically harmonic, i.e. in the kernel of
D_{\zeta;V}

collapse in the adiabatic limit
, and hence the ”classical” operator norm of
D^{t}
is not
t
-independently bounded from below. This consequential unboundedness from above of a right-inverse is problematic for the application in gluing analysis and hence we need to adapted the used norms to compensate for this collapsing phenomena. The construction of these norms is inspired by work of Walpuski
[
Wal17a
,
Wal17b
]
and Platt
[
Pla20
]
.

We will now outline the structure of the paper:

•

In section
2
we review Riemannian orbifolds and their resolutions. We will categories singularity models into type (I) and type (II). Resolutions of type (I) singularities will be the main interest of this paper. These resolutions will be constructed via
asymptotically conical fibrations (ACF)
, i.e. families of asymptotically conical spaces, resolving the
conical fibred (CF)
normal cone bundle of a singular stratum. We will continue by sketching how certain classes of type (II) singularities can be resolved using similar techniques.

•

In section
3
we will review the structure of Hermitian Dirac bundles over Riemannian fibre bundles by following the work of Gromoll and Walshap in
[
GW09
]
and Berline, Getzler and Vergne in
[
BGV92
]
. In particular, we will show that the CF Dirac operator on the normal cone bundle
X_{0}
, and subsequently the resolved ACF Dirac operator on
X_{\zeta}
can be written as the sum of a “well behaving” family of “horizontal” Dirac operators and
t^{-1}\cdot D_{V}
where the latter is the Dirac operator associated to the vertical Hermitian Dirac bundle.

•

In section
4
we revisit linear gluing methods in subsection
4.1
and introduce the boundary operator
B
in subsection
4.2
. Using an elaborated version of separation of variables, we obtain a
polyhomogeneous expansion of the kernel and cokernel
elements of the Dirac operators involved in this construction. Using the gained insights, we discuss the right definition of weighted function spaces to mimic the gluing method outlined at the beginning of subject section.

•

In section
5
we discuss the analytic realisation of the boundary operator. We explicitly compute its polyhomogenous solutions and by imposing ASP-type boundary conditions we can realise the boundary operator as an isomorphism of Banach spaces.

Proposition
5.2
.

Let us assume that
\beta\notin\mathcal{C}(\widehat{D}_{0})
. The space
C^{k+1,\alpha}_{CF;\beta}(\widehat{D}_{0};+)
is complete and

\displaystyle\widehat{D}_{0}:C^{k+1,\alpha}_{CF;\beta}(\widehat{D}_{0};+)\rightarrow C^{k,\alpha}_{CF;\beta-1}(X_{0},E_{0})

is an isomorphism of Banach spaces.

•

In section
6
we use the derived results and establish an elliptic theory for CSF Dirac operators on orbifolds. We establish a uniform CSF-Schauder estimate in weighted function spaces.

Proposition
6.2

(CSF-Schauder Estimates)
.

Let
\Phi\in C^{k+1,\alpha}_{CSF;\beta;\epsilon}(X,E;+)

\displaystyle\left|\left|\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{k+1,\alpha}_{CSF;\beta;\epsilon}}\lesssim

\displaystyle\left|\left|D\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{k,\alpha}_{CSF;\beta-1;\epsilon}}+\left|\left|\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{0}_{CSF;\beta;\epsilon}}

holds.

By improving on this Schauder estimate we are able to prove that the chosen realisation of
D
is Fredholm unless
\beta\in\mathcal{C}(\widehat{D}_{0})
. Further its index satisfies a wall-crossing formula.

Proposition
6.3
.

Let
\beta\notin\mathcal{C}(\widehat{D}_{0})
. The map

\displaystyle D:C^{k+1,\alpha}_{CSF;\beta;\epsilon}(X,E;+)\rightarrow C^{k,\alpha}_{CSF;\beta-1;\epsilon}(X,E)

is Fredholm and for
\beta_{2}<\beta_{1}

\displaystyle{\mathrm{ind}}_{\beta_{2}}(D)-{\mathrm{ind}}_{\beta_{1}}(D)=\sum_{\beta_{2}<\lambda<\beta_{1}}d_{\lambda+\frac{m-1}{2}-\delta(E)}(\widehat{D}_{0}).

•

In section
7
we will study the family of ACF-Dirac operators
\widehat{D}^{t}_{\zeta}
. Such families of Hermitian Dirac bundles are the noncompact analogue of the adiabatic families of Dirac bundles studied by Jean-Michel Bismut
[
Bis86
,
BC89
,
BL91
,
BL95
]
, Sebastian Goette
[
Goe14
]
and others. We will build upon their ideas and analyse the family of Dirac operators
\widehat{D}^{t}_{\zeta}
close to their adiabatic limit. We identify the
adiabatic kernel of
\widehat{\textbf{D}}^{\textbf{t}}_{\zeta}
with the kernel of the adiabatic residue

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{K};\beta}:\Gamma(S,\mathcal{K}_{AC;\beta}(\pi_{\zeta}/\nu_{\zeta}))\rightarrow\Gamma(S,\mathcal{K}_{AC;\beta}(\pi_{\zeta}/\nu_{\zeta}))

and the
adiabatic cokernel of
\widehat{\textbf{D}}^{\textbf{t}}_{\zeta}
with the cokernel of the operator

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{C}o\mathcal{K};\beta}:\Gamma(S,\mathcal{C}o\mathcal{K}_{AC;\beta}(\pi_{\zeta}/\nu_{\zeta}))\rightarrow\Gamma(S,\mathcal{C}o\mathcal{K}_{AC;\beta}(\pi_{\zeta}/\nu_{\zeta}))

These operators are elliptic,
t
-independent Dirac type operators on the singular stratum acting on sections of the finite dimensional Hermitian Dirac bundle of vertical harmonic spinors
2
2

2

i.e. the bundle of (co)kernels of
D_{\zeta;V}
.
.

We will further introduce the notion of isentropic families of Dirac operators on
X_{\zeta}
. An isentropic process is an adiabatic process that is reversible, in the same manner an isentropic operator can modelled by its adiabatic limit. For these isentropic Dirac operators, the space of adiabatic harmonic spinors is isomorphic to the space of harmonic spinors close to the adiabatic limit.

We proceed by following ideas of Walpuski
[
Wal17a
,
Wal17b
]
, Platt
[
Pla20
]
, Degeratu and Mazzeo
[
DM18
]
we construct adapted norms and we show that
uniform estimates for the operator bounds of the family of Dirac operators
hold on the family of the resolutions. These adapted norms are of the form

\displaystyle\left|\left|\Phi\right|\right|_{\mathfrak{D}^{k+1,\alpha}_{ACF;\beta;t}}=\left|\left|\pi_{\mathcal{C}o\mathcal{I};\beta}\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{k+1,\alpha}_{ACF;\beta;t}}+t^{-\kappa}\left|\left|\varpi_{\mathcal{K};\beta}\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{k+1,\alpha}_{t}}

\displaystyle\left|\left|\Phi\right|\right|_{\mathfrak{C}^{k,\alpha}_{ACF;\beta-1;t}}=\left|\left|\pi_{\mathcal{I};\beta}\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{k,\alpha}_{ACF;\beta-1;t}}+t^{-\kappa}\left|\left|\varpi_{\mathcal{C}o\mathcal{K};\beta}\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{k,\alpha}_{t}}

where
\pi_{\mathcal{C}o\mathcal{I};\beta}
,
\pi_{\mathcal{K};\beta}
,
\pi_{\mathcal{I};\beta}
and
\pi_{\mathcal{C}o\mathcal{K};\beta}
denote the projection onto the vertical coimage, kernel, image and cokernel of the vertical Dirac operator
D_{\zeta;V}
. We define the space

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}^{k+1,\alpha}_{ACF;\beta;t}(\widehat{D}^{t}_{\zeta}):=\left({\mathrm{dom}}_{\mathfrak{D}^{k+1,\alpha}_{ACF;\beta;t}}(\widehat{D}^{t}_{\zeta}),\left|\left|.\right|\right|_{\mathfrak{D}^{k+1,\alpha}_{ACF;\beta;t}(\widehat{D}^{t}_{\zeta})}\right)

equipped with the graph norm

\displaystyle\left|\left|\Phi\right|\right|_{\mathfrak{D}^{k+1,\alpha}_{ACF;\beta;t}(\widehat{D}^{t}_{\zeta})}=\left|\left|\Phi\right|\right|_{\mathfrak{D}^{k+1,\alpha}_{ACF;\beta;t}}+\left|\left|\widehat{D}^{t}_{\zeta}\Phi\right|\right|_{\mathfrak{C}^{k,\alpha}_{ACF;\beta-1;t}}.

Proposition
7.7
.

The space
\mathfrak{D}^{k+1,\alpha}_{ACF;\beta;t}(\widehat{D}^{t}_{\zeta})
is a Banach space and the map

\displaystyle\widehat{D}^{t}_{\zeta}:\mathfrak{D}^{k+1,\alpha}_{ACF;\beta;t}(\widehat{D}^{t}_{\zeta})\rightarrow\mathfrak{C}^{k,\alpha}_{ACF;\beta-1;t}

is Fredholm. The kernel and cokernel are smooth, i.e. independent of
\alpha
and
k
. Moreover,

\displaystyle{\mathrm{ind}}_{\beta}(\widehat{D}^{t}_{\zeta})=\mathfrak{ind}_{\beta}(\widehat{D}^{t}_{\zeta})={\mathrm{dim}}({\mathrm{ker}}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{K};\beta}))-{\mathrm{dim}}({\mathrm{coker}}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{C}o\mathcal{K};\beta}))

and hence if
\beta_{2}<\beta_{1}
is a critical rate, then

\displaystyle{\mathrm{ind}}_{\beta_{2}}(\widehat{D}^{t}_{\zeta})-{\mathrm{ind}}_{\beta_{1}}(\widehat{D}^{t}_{\zeta})=\sum_{\beta_{2}<\lambda<\beta_{1}}d_{\lambda+\frac{m-1}{2}-\delta(E_{0})}(\widehat{D}_{0})

Furthermore, we are able to improve on the weighted Schauder estimate and are show that the operator
\widehat{D}^{t}_{\zeta}
is
uniformally bounded from below
on the complement of the adiabatic kernel.

Proposition
7.8
.

Assume that
-\iota<\kappa<\pi
. Then the operator

\displaystyle\widehat{D}^{t}_{\zeta}:\mathfrak{D}^{k+1,\alpha}_{ACF;\beta;t}(\widehat{D}^{t}_{\zeta})\rightarrow\mathfrak{C}^{k,\alpha}_{ACF;\beta-1;t}

satisfies

\displaystyle\left|\left|\Phi\right|\right|_{\mathfrak{D}^{k+1,\alpha}_{ACF;\beta;t}}\lesssim\left|\left|\widehat{D}^{t}_{\zeta}\Phi\right|\right|_{\mathfrak{C}^{k,\alpha}_{ACF;\beta-1;t}}+t^{-\kappa}\cdot\left|\left|\pi_{\mathcal{K};\beta}\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{0}_{t}}

Further, let
\Phi\in\mathfrak{Ker}_{\beta}(\widehat{D}^{t}_{\zeta})^{\perp}={\mathrm{ker}}(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{K};\beta})^{\perp}
. Then

\displaystyle\left|\left|\Phi\right|\right|_{\mathfrak{D}^{k+1,\alpha}_{ACF;\beta;t}}\lesssim

\displaystyle\left|\left|\widehat{D}^{t}_{\zeta}\Phi\right|\right|_{\mathfrak{C}^{k,\alpha}_{ACF;\beta-1;t}}.

In section
8
we will state and prove the main theorem of this paper. We begin by define the approximate kernel and cokernel of
D^{t}
by

\displaystyle{\mathrm{xker}}_{\beta}(D^{t})=\begin{array}[]{c}{\mathrm{ker}}\left(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{K};\beta}:C^{k+1,\alpha}_{t}\left(S,\mathcal{K}_{AC;\beta}(\pi_{\zeta}/\nu_{\zeta})\right)\rightarrow C^{k,\alpha}_{t}\left(S,\mathcal{K}_{AC;\beta}(\pi_{\zeta}/\nu_{\zeta})\right)\right)\\
\oplus\\
{\mathrm{ker}}\left(D:C^{\bullet,\alpha}_{CSF;\beta;\epsilon}(D)\rightarrow C^{\bullet-1,\alpha}_{CSF;\beta-1;\epsilon}\right)\end{array}

and

\hskip 42.67912pt{\mathrm{xcoker}}_{\beta}(D^{t})=\begin{array}[]{c}{\mathrm{coker}}\left(\mathfrak{D}_{\mathcal{C}o\mathcal{K};\beta}:C^{k+1,\alpha}_{t}\left(S,\mathcal{C}o\mathcal{K}_{AC;\beta}(\pi_{\zeta}/\nu_{\zeta})\right)\rightarrow C^{k,\alpha}_{t}\left(S,\mathcal{C}o\mathcal{K}_{AC;\beta}(\pi_{\zeta}/\nu_{\zeta})\right)\right)\\
\oplus\\
{\mathrm{coker}}\left(D:C^{\bullet,\alpha}_{CSF;\beta;\epsilon}(D)\rightarrow C^{\bullet-1,\alpha}_{CSF;\beta-1;\epsilon}\right).\end{array}

Further, we will introduce function spaces of sections of
E^{t}
by interpolating the ACF and CSF-norms. Using the results of the prior sections we are able to state and to prove the main theorem:

Theorem
8.1
.

The operator
D^{t}
satisfies

\displaystyle\left|\left|\Phi\right|\right|_{\mathfrak{D}^{k+1,\alpha}_{\beta;t}}\lesssim\left|\left|D^{t}\Phi\right|\right|_{\mathfrak{C}^{k,\alpha}_{\beta-1;t}}

for all
\Phi\in{\mathrm{xker}}(D^{t})^{\perp}
. Moreover, there exists an exact sequence

(3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{ker}}(D^{t})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{xker}}_{\beta}(D^{t})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{xcoker}}_{\beta}(D^{t})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{coker}}(D^{t})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{t}_{\beta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{ob}}^{t}_{\beta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{t}_{\beta}}

which implies the existence of a uniformally bounded right-inverse of
D^{t}
if
{\mathrm{ob}}^{t}_{\beta}=0
.

By taking the Euler characteristic of the sequence, we deduce that
{\mathrm{ind}}\circ\cup^{t}=+\circ\mathfrak{ind}
, i.e.

\displaystyle{\mathrm{ind}}(D^{t})={\mathrm{ind}}_{\beta}(D)+\mathfrak{ind}_{\beta}(\widehat{D}^{0}_{\zeta}).

•

In appendix
A
we will review the adiabatic structure of Riemannian fibrations and list how the Levi-Civita connection and Riemannian curvature tensors decompose with respect to a given Ehresmann connection.

•

In appendix
B
we are discussing the spectrum of Dirac operators on homology spheres. This is essential in the discussion of concrete examples of the uniform elliptic theory we construct in this paper, as the spectrum of the vertical link Dirac operator, i.e. a Dirac operator on a homology sphere, determines the set of critical rates
\mathcal{C}(\widehat{D}_{0})
for which the ACF-CF-CSF Fredholm theory fails.

2.
Riemannian Orbifolds

In the following section we will briefly discuss how one should think of Riemannian orbifolds by following the work of Moerdijk in
[
Moe02
]
. In the approach the category of orbifolds is associated with the category of proper foliation groupoids, up to Morita equivalence. By representing an orbifold by a Lie groupoid, the right notions of tangent bundle and normal bundle can be understood in a more natural way then by the usual chart based approach.

2.1.
The Category of Orbifolds

Definition 2.1
.

Let
X^{\bullet}
be a Lie groupoid, i.e. a groupoid
3
3

3

A category such that each morphism is an isomorphism.
object in
\textbf{C}^{\infty}\hskip-2.84544pt\textbf{Mfd}
.

•

X^{\bullet}
is called a proper Lie groupoid if
(s,t):X^{1}\rightarrow X^{0}\times X^{0}
is a proper map. In particular, every isotropy group is a compact Lie group.

•

X^{\bullet}
is called a foliation groupoid if every isotropy group is discrete.

•

X^{\bullet}
is called étale if
s
and
t
are local diffeomorphisms.

Let
X^{\bullet}
be an étale Lie groupoid. Then every arrow
\phi:x\to y
in
X^{\bullet}
induces a well-defined germ of a diffeomorphism
\tilde{\phi}:(U_{x},x)\rightarrow(V_{y},y)
, as
\tilde{\phi}=t\circ\hat{\phi}
, where
\hat{\phi}:U_{X}\rightarrow X^{1}
is an
s:X^{1}\rightarrow X^{0}
section, defined on a sufficiently small neighbourhood
U_{x}
of
x
.

•

X^{\bullet}
is called faithfull, if for each point
x\in X^{0}
, the homomorphism
G_{x}\rightarrow{\mathrm{Diff}}_{x}(X^{0})
is injective.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
X^{\bullet}
be a Lie groupoid. A left
X^{\bullet}
-space
E
is a smooth manifold,
E
, equipped with an action of
X^{\bullet}
. Such an action is given by the smooth morphisms

\displaystyle\pi:E\rightarrow X^{0}\hskip 28.45274pt\mu_{L}:X^{0}\times_{s,\pi}E\rightarrow E\hskip 28.45274pt(\mu_{R}:E\times_{\pi;t}X^{0}\rightarrow E)

satisfying
\pi(\phi.e)=s(\phi)
,
1_{x}.e=e
and
\eta.(\phi.e)=(\eta\phi).e
. For each
X^{\bullet}
space
E
, we can form the translation groupoid
(X\ltimes E)^{\bullet}
, whose objects are points in
E
and whose morphisms
\phi:e\rightarrow e^{\prime}
are morphisms
\phi:\pi(e)\rightarrow\pi(e^{\prime})
in
X^{1}
with
\phi.e=e^{\prime}
. There exists an obvious homomorphism
\pi_{E}:(X\ltimes E)^{\bullet}\rightarrow X^{\bullet}
of orbifolds.

Remark 1
.

Naturally by replacing the left by right actions in the above definition we can define right
X^{\bullet}
-spaces.

\heartsuit

Definition 2.3
.

A right/left-
X^{\bullet}
-
Y^{\bullet}
-bibundle
Z
, is given by smooth maps
\alpha_{X}:Z\rightarrow X^{0}
and
\alpha_{Y}:Z\rightarrow Y^{0}
called the moment maps, such that both
\alpha_{X}
is a right/left
X^{\bullet}
-space and
\alpha_{Y}
is a right/left
Y^{\bullet}
-space, i.e.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X^{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y^{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X^{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y^{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}

Definition 2.4
.

A generalised morphism

\displaystyle[Z]:X^{\bullet}\rightsquigarrow Y^{\bullet}

is given by an isomorphism class of right
X^{\bullet}
-
Y^{\bullet}
-bibundles.

Definition 2.5
.

A Lie groupoid
X^{\bullet}
is Morita equivalent to Lie groupoid
Y^{\bullet}
if there exists a generalised morphism

\displaystyle[Z]:X^{\bullet}\rightsquigarrow Y^{\bullet}

which is also a left
X^{\bullet}
-
Y^{\bullet}
-bibundle.

Remark 2
.

Recall that
X^{\bullet}
and
Y^{\bullet}
Morita equivalence is equivalent to the existence of a third Lie groupoid
Z^{\bullet}
and equivalences

\displaystyle Z^{\bullet}\xrightarrow{\phi^{\bullet}}X^{\bullet}\hskip 28.45274pt\text{and}\hskip 28.45274ptZ^{\bullet}\xrightarrow{\psi^{\bullet}}Y^{\bullet},

i.e. the maps
t\circ{\mathrm{pr}}_{1}:X^{1}\times_{s,\phi^{0};X^{0}}Z^{0}\rightarrow X^{0}
and
t\circ{\mathrm{pr}}_{1}:Y^{1}\times_{s,\psi^{0};Y^{0}}Z^{0}\rightarrow Y^{0}
are surjective submersions and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{0}\times X^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z^{0}\times Z^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{0}\times Y^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s\times t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s\times t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s\times t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{0}\times\phi^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi^{0}\times\psi^{0}}

are pullback squares.

\heartsuit

Definition 2.6
.

A smooth
n
-dimensional orbifold
X^{\bullet}
is the equivalence class of a smooth, proper, foliation Lie groupoid
s,t:X^{1}\rightrightarrows X^{0}
of dimension
n
up to Morita equivalence. Given such an equivalence class, we will write

\displaystyle[X^{0}/X^{1}]:=[X^{\bullet}].

A morphism of orbifolds, is given by an equivalence class of generalised morphism

\displaystyle[[Z]]:[X^{\bullet}]\rightsquigarrow[Y^{\bullet}].

Remark 3
.

In every equivalence class of an orbifold
[X^{\bullet}]\subset\textbf{LieGrpd}
there exists a proper, étale groupoid representing the orbifold.

\heartsuit

Remark 4
.

Notice that the orbit space
X^{0}/X^{1}
just carries the data of a topological space. The stacky quotient
[X^{0}/X^{1}]
“remembers” the isotropy.

\heartsuit

Corollary 2.1
.

Let
X^{\bullet}
be a smooth
n
-dimensional orbifold represented by a proper étale Lie groupoid. For every open subset
U\subset X^{0}
we write
(X|_{U})^{\bullet}
for the subgroupid whose objects are
U
. Since
X
is assumed to be proper and étale, for
x\in X
there exists an arbitrarily small neighbourhood
U_{x}
such that
(X|_{U_{x}})^{\bullet}
is isomorphic to the action groupoid
({\mathrm{Isot}}_{x}\ltimes U_{x})^{\bullet}
.

Remark 5
.

This corollary is usually seen as the definition of an orbifold via orbifold charts, i.e.
X
being a topological space covered by charts of the form
({\mathrm{Isot}}_{x}\ltimes U_{x})^{\bullet}
subject to gluing conditions.

\heartsuit

Remark 6
.

Orbifolds are examples of stratified spaces. The stratification of
X^{\bullet}
consists of the suborbifolds
S^{\bullet}\subset X^{\bullet}
for which
{\mathrm{Isot}}_{s}\cong{\mathrm{Isot}}_{s^{\prime}}
for all
s,s^{\prime}\in S^{\bullet}
. In particular,

\displaystyle X=X^{reg}\cup\bigcup_{[\Gamma_{\alpha}]}X_{\alpha}

where the union runs over all isomorphisms classes
[\Gamma_{\alpha}]
of finite subgroups of
{\mathrm{GL}}(n)
.
We can further define a partial order on the set
\{X_{\alpha}\}
by

\displaystyle X_{\alpha}\leq X_{\beta}

if
\Gamma_{\beta}\subset\Gamma_{\alpha}
. Notice that since
\{X_{\alpha}\}
defines a stratification,
X_{\beta}\leq X_{\alpha}
implies
X_{\beta}\subset\overline{X_{\alpha}}
. Hence, we can define a prestratification of
X
by
X_{\tilde{\alpha}}=\{\bigcup_{\beta\leq\alpha}X_{\beta}\}_{\alpha}
. In particular, we say that two connected singular strata
X_{\alpha}
and
X_{\alpha^{\prime}}
intersect in a singular stratum
X_{\beta}
, if
X_{\beta}\leq X_{\alpha}
and
X_{\beta}\leq X_{\alpha^{\prime}}
or equivalently

\displaystyle X_{\tilde{\alpha}}\cap X_{\tilde{\alpha}^{\prime}}=X_{\beta}.

\heartsuit

Proposition 2.1
.

Every
X_{\alpha}
is a smooth (open) manifold and
\overline{X_{\alpha}}=X_{\alpha}\sqcup\bigsqcup_{\beta\leq\alpha}X_{\beta}
.

Definition 2.7
.

We define the depth
{\mathrm{depth}}(X_{\alpha})
of a singular stratum
X_{\alpha}
, as the length of the longest chain of inclusions of singular strata, i.e.

\displaystyle{\mathrm{depth}}(X_{\alpha})=\max\{i\in\mathbb{N}|X_{\alpha}=X_{\beta_{0}}\leq...\leq X_{\beta_{i}}=X^{reg}|X_{\beta_{k}}\neq X_{\beta_{k-1}}\}.

2.2.
Geometric Structures on Orbifolds

In this subsection we will introduce geometric structures on orbifolds such as vector bundles, principal bundles and Riemannian orbifold metrics.

Remark 7
.

Notice that on the level of groupoids, the fibre over
x\in X^{0}
is
\pi^{-1}(x)
, at the level of orbit spaces
|X\ltimes E|\rightarrow|X|
the fibres are
\pi^{-1}(x)/{\mathrm{Isot}}_{x}
.

\heartsuit

Remark 8
.

Naturally the tangent bundle of an orbifold
TX^{\bullet}
is a
X^{\bullet}
-vector bundle, i.e.

\displaystyle\pi_{X}:TX^{0}\rightarrow X^{0}\hskip 28.45274pt\mu_{TX}:X^{1}\times_{s,\pi_{TX^{0}}}TX^{0}\rightarrow TX^{0}

where
\mu(g\times V_{x})=Tg(V_{x})\in T_{y}X^{0}
and
\phi:(U_{x},x)\rightarrow(V_{y},y)
. Furthermore, if
S^{\bullet}
is a singular stratum of isotropy
\Gamma
, then the normal bundle
NS^{\bullet}\rightarrow S^{\bullet}
of
S^{\bullet}
, is a
S^{\bullet}
-vector bundle, but
N|S|\rightarrow|S|
is a (quasi) conical bundle.

\heartsuit

Definition 2.8
.

The frame bundle
Fr_{X^{\bullet}}
of an
n
-dimensional orbifold is the
X^{\bullet}
-principal
{\mathrm{GL}}(n)
-bundle. It is given by

\displaystyle f_{X}:Fr_{X^{0}}\rightarrow X^{0}\hskip 28.45274pt\mu_{Fr_{X}}:X^{\times}_{s,\phi_{X^{0}}}Fr_{X^{0}}\rightarrow Fr_{X^{0}}

where
\mu_{Fr_{X}}(\psi,f_{x})=T\psi\circ f_{x}
.

Proposition 2.2
.

Let
X
be an orbifold represented by a Lie groupoid
X^{\bullet}
, let
\Pi:X^{\bullet}\rightarrow[X^{0}/X^{1}]
denote the quotient map and let
S\subset X
denote a singular strata of isotropy
{\mathrm{Isot}}_{s}\cong\Gamma
. There exists a covering map
{\mathrm{isot}}_{S}:{\mathrm{Isot}}(S)\rightarrow S
such that the Lie groupoid
\Pi^{-1}(S)
is Morita equivalent to the Lie groupoid
s=t={\mathrm{isot}}_{S}
.

Proof.

Let
\Pi^{-1}(S)\subset X^{\bullet}
denote the subgroupoid of
X^{\bullet}
corresponding to
S
. Then

\displaystyle\widetilde{{\mathrm{Isot}}}(S)=\Pi^{-1}(S)^{1}\times_{s;t}\Pi^{-1}(S)^{1}\rightarrow\Pi^{-1}(S)^{0}

defines a
\Pi^{-1}(S)^{\bullet}
bundle that descends to the quotient

\displaystyle{\mathrm{isot}}_{S}:{\mathrm{Isot}}(S):=[\widetilde{{\mathrm{Isot}}}(S)/\Pi^{-1}(S)^{1}]\rightarrow[\Pi^{-1}(S)^{0}/\Pi^{-1}(S)^{1}]=S.

∎

Proposition 2.3
.

Let
S^{\bullet}\subset X^{\bullet}
be a singular strata of isotropy type
\Gamma
. Then there exists a reduction
N_{\Gamma}\hookrightarrow{\mathrm{GL}}(n)
reduction of the restriction of the frame bundle
Fr_{X^{\bullet}}
to
S
.

Proof.

Let
X^{\bullet}
be a Lie groupoid representing the orbifold
[X^{0}/X^{1}]
. Let
S^{\bullet}
denote the sub Lie groupoid corresponding to
S\subset[X^{0}/X^{1}]
in the quotient space. Notice that the
X^{\bullet}
-frame bundle, restricts to a
{\mathrm{GL}}(n)
-principal bundle of
S^{\bullet}
, i.e.

\displaystyle f_{X}|_{S}:Fr_{X^{0}}|_{S^{0}}\rightarrow S^{0}\hskip 28.45274pt\mu_{Fr_{X}|_{S}}:S^{1}\times_{s,\phi_{X^{0}}|_{S^{0}}}Fr_{X^{0}}|_{S^{0}}\rightarrow Fr_{X^{0}}|_{S^{0}}.

We know that there exists a short exact sequence of
S^{\bullet}
-vector bundles

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TS^{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX^{\bullet}|_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces NS^{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

In particular, there exists a natural reduction to frames

\displaystyle f_{x}:H\oplus V\rightarrow TX^{0}

such that
p\circ f_{x}|_{V}\in{\mathrm{Fr}}(NS^{\bullet})
. A moment of thought reveals this reduction can be represented by smooth principal-
N_{\Gamma}({\mathrm{GL}}(n))
-bundle
\hat{F}_{S}\rightarrow S
.
∎

Definition 2.9
.

A
G
-structure on an orbifold, is given by a
G\hookrightarrow{\mathrm{GL}}(n)
-reduction of the frame bundle. In particular, all isotropy groups need to be conjugated to subgroups of
G
.

Remark 9
.

A Riemannian orbifold is given by the
{\mathrm{O}}(n)
-reduction of an orbifold. Equivalently, this is Riemannian structure on
X^{0}
, such that

\displaystyle g_{x}=\phi^{*}g_{y}

for all
\phi:(U_{x},x)\rightarrow(V_{y},y)
.

\heartsuit

Corollary 2.2
.

Let
X
be a Riemannian orbifold and
S
a singular strata of isotropy
\Gamma
. Then
F_{S}\rightarrow S
is a torsion-free extension of the frame bundle of
S
, i.e. a

\displaystyle N_{\Gamma}({\mathrm{O}}(n))\twoheadrightarrow{\mathrm{O}}(n-m)

extension

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Fr_{{\mathrm{O}},S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}

such that the Levi-Civita connection of
X

\varphi^{LC,X}\in\mathcal{A}(F_{S})
is a torsion-free extension of the Levi-Civita connection
\varphi^{LC,S}\in\mathcal{A}(Fr_{{\mathrm{O}},S})
on
S
.

2.3.
Resolutions of Riemannian Orbifolds

In the following subsection, we will introduce the concept of Riemannian orbifold resolution as covered in
[
PTW17
]
.

Definition 2.10
.

Let
(X,g)
be a Riemannian orbifold represented by a Riemannian Lie groupoid
(X^{\bullet},g^{\bullet})
Morita equivalent to a proper foliation Lie groupoid. A resolution of
(X^{\bullet},g^{\bullet})
is given by Riemannian Lie groupoid
(\widetilde{X}^{\bullet},\widetilde{g}^{\bullet})
Morita equivalent to a regular, foliation Lie groupoid; and a Riemannian submersion of Lie groupoids

\displaystyle\rho:(\widetilde{X}^{\bullet},\widetilde{g}^{\bullet})\rightarrow(X^{\bullet},g^{\bullet}),

which is an isomorphism almost everywhere and an isometry on
X^{reg}\backslash U
for an open proper subset
U\subset X
containing
X^{sing}
. A morphisms of resolutions of Riemannian orbifolds

\displaystyle(\widetilde{X}^{\bullet},\widetilde{g}^{\bullet})\rightsquigarrow(\widetilde{X}^{\prime\bullet},\widetilde{g}^{\prime\bullet})

is a Riemannian orbifold morphism, such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\widetilde{X}^{\bullet},\widetilde{g}^{\bullet})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\widetilde{X}^{\prime\bullet},\widetilde{g}^{\prime\bullet})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X^{\bullet},g^{\bullet})}

We will write
\textbf{Res}(X,g)
for the category of Riemannian orbifold resolutions.

Lemma 2.1

(
[
PTW17
]
)
.

For every Riemannian orbifold, the category
(X,g)
is nontrivial.

We will construct an example prominently used in incomplete edge calculus.
[
AGR16
]

Let
(X,g)
be a Riemannian orbifold, represented by a Riemannian proper foliating Lie groupoid
(X^{\bullet},g^{\bullet})
and let

\displaystyle\pi:(X^{0},g^{0})\rightarrow([X^{0}/X^{1}],g)=(X,g)

denote the orbit map. Let
\pi^{-1}X^{sing}\subset X^{0}
denote the preimage of the singular strata of
X
and let us denote by

\displaystyle\beta^{0}_{e}:([X^{0}:\pi^{-1}X^{sing}],g^{0}_{e})\dashrightarrow(X^{0},g^{0})

the real blow-up of
X^{0}
along
\pi^{-1}X^{sing}
, i.e. the smooth manifold

\displaystyle X^{0}\backslash\pi^{-1}X^{sing}\cup_{exp_{g^{0}}}\mathbb{S}N\pi^{-1}X^{sing}

equipped with the minimal smooth structure making the blow down map

\displaystyle\beta_{e}:[X^{0}:\pi^{-1}X^{sing}]\dashrightarrow X^{0}

smooth. Further
g^{0}_{e}
denotes the blow up of
g_{0}
. As proven in
[
PTW17
]
, the action of the morphisms
X^{1}
lifts to
([X^{0}:\pi^{-1}X^{sing}],g^{0}_{e})
, endowing

\displaystyle([X^{\bullet}:\pi^{-1}X^{sing}],g^{\bullet}_{e})

with the structure of a Riemannian, foliating Lie groupoid.

Definition 2.11
.

We define the real blow-up of
(X,g)

\displaystyle\beta_{e}:([X:X^{sing}],g_{e})\dashrightarrow(X,g)

as the orbit space of
([X^{\bullet}:\pi^{-1}X^{sing}],g^{\bullet}_{e})
.

Lemma 2.2

(
[
PTW17
]
)
.

The real blow up
\beta_{e}:([X:X^{sing}],g_{e})\dashrightarrow(X,g)
is a smooth Riemannian manifold with boundary and
\beta_{e}
is a smooth submersion.

2.4.
Riemannian Orbifolds as Iterated Edge Spaces

In this section, we provide a brief review of Riemannian orbifolds and their interpretation as iterated edge spaces.

We will discuss the concept of iterated edge spaces as used by Mazzeo in
[
Maz91
,
MV14
]
from the perspective of Riemannian orbifolds (see
[
Pfl01
]
). Instead of locally quasi conical spaces as local models of singular strata we will use (quasi) conically fibred space. These specification emphasise the impotence of the singular stratum in resolution constructions.

Definition 2.12
.

Let
(X,g)
be a Riemannian orbifold and let
X^{sing}
denote the union of its singular strata. Let
S
be a stratum whose isotropy group is conjugated to a finite group
\Gamma\subset{\mathrm{O}}(n)
. We say

•

X
is of
singularity type (I)
at
S
, if
X
at
S\subset X
is locally modelled on

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{n-m}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}/\Gamma

such that
\Gamma
acts freely on
\mathbb{R}^{m}\backslash\{0\}
.

•

X
is of
singularity type (IIa)
at
S
, if
X
at
S\subset X
is locally modelled on

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{n-\sum_{i}m_{i}}\times\bigtimes_{i}\mathbb{R}^{m_{i}}/\Gamma_{i}

such that
\Gamma_{i}
acts freely on
\mathbb{R}^{m_{i}}\backslash\{0\}
.

•

X
is of
singularity type (IIb)
at
S
, if it is neither type (I) nor type (IIa)

Remark 10
.

Let
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
be two singular strata of isotropy
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
. Then the intersection
S_{3}=\overline{S_{1}}\cap\overline{S_{2}}
is a singular strata of isotropy
\Gamma_{1}\Gamma_{2}\subset\Gamma_{3}
and
X
is singular of type
(IIa)
or
(IIb)
.

\heartsuit

Let
i:(S,g_{S})\hookrightarrow(X,g)
be a connected singular stratum of codimension
m
, whose isotropy group is
\Gamma\subset{\mathrm{O}}(m)
. Let further
{\mathrm{Isot}}(S)\rightarrow S
denote the
\Gamma
-bundle associated to
S\subset X
and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TS}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{*}TX}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces NS}

define its normal bundle. We will think of
NS
as a vector bundle
\nu:NS\rightarrow S
with a fibrewise
\Gamma
-action

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Isot}}(S)\times_{S}NS\rightarrow NS.

We define the normal cone bundle to be the conically fibred space

\displaystyle\nu_{0}:X_{0}=NS/{\mathrm{Isot}}(S)\rightarrow S.

Let further
F_{S}
be the natural
N_{\Gamma}
-reduction of the
{\mathrm{O}}(n)
frame bundle
Fr_{{\mathrm{O}}(n)}(X)
of
(X,g)
restricted to
S
. Define the associated (orbifold) bundle

\displaystyle Y:=F_{S}\times_{N_{\Gamma}}\mathbb{S}^{m-1}/\Gamma\xrightarrow{\vartheta}S.

Notice, that when ever
(S,g_{S})
is of type (I), the bundle
Y
is smooth. Locally, the blow-down map
\beta_{e}:[X:X^{sing}]\dashrightarrow X
is given

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[X_{0}:S\right]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{0}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{e}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{e}}

Following
[
Maz91
]
we define a vector bundle on
[X:X^{sing}]
that realise the orbifold structure.

Definition 2.13
.

We define the incomplete edge-forms on
[X:X^{sing}]
to be the graded subalgebra

\displaystyle\Omega^{\bullet}_{ie}([X:X^{sing}])=\{\omega\in\Omega^{\bullet}([X:X^{sing}])|{\mathrm{res}}_{X^{sing}}(\omega)\in\vartheta^{*}\Omega^{\bullet}(X^{sing})\}\subset\Omega^{\bullet}([X:X^{sing}])

which can be realised as the exterior algebra of section of the incomplete edge-cotangent bundle

\displaystyle\prescript{ie}{}{}T^{*}[X:X^{sing}]\rightarrow[X:X^{sing}].

Remark 11
.

The blow-down map
\beta_{e}
induces a Lie algebroid structure on the dual to
\prescript{ie}{}{}T^{*}[X:X^{sing}]
, via the anchor map

\displaystyle\prescript{ie}{}{}\alpha:\prescript{ie}{}{}T[X:X^{sing}]\rightarrow T[X:X^{sing}].

\heartsuit

There exists an exact sequence of orbifold vector bundles on
X_{0}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces VX_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TX_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{0}^{*}TS.}

or equivalently a splitting of the bundle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prescript{ie}{}{}V[X_{0}:S]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prescript{ie}{}{}T[X_{0}:S]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{0}^{*}TS.}

The Riemannian orbifold structure
g
induces a splitting

\displaystyle TX_{0}\cong H_{0}\oplus VX_{0}\cong\nu^{*}_{0}TS\oplus\nu^{*}_{0}NS

and a Riemannian orbifold structure on the normal cone bundle
X_{0}
such that

\displaystyle g_{0}=\nu^{*}i^{*}g=\nu^{*}_{0}g_{S}+g_{0;V}\in\Gamma\left(X_{0},{\mathrm{Sym}}^{2}H_{0}^{*}\oplus{\mathrm{Sym}}^{2}V^{*}X_{0}\right).

Equivalently, we can think of a Riemannian orbifold structure on
(X_{0},g_{0})
as a real Hermitian Lie algebroid structure on
(\prescript{ie}{}{}T[X_{0}:S],g_{0})
.

Notice that the compact (orbifold) fibration
\vartheta:Y\rightarrow S
is equipped with an Ehresmann connection
H_{Y}
such that the connection
H_{0}
on
X_{0}
can be identified with

\displaystyle({\mathrm{id}}\oplus A_{H_{\overline{\vartheta}}}):TX_{0}\cong\underline{\mathbb{R}}\oplus TY\rightarrow\underline{\mathbb{R}}\oplus VY.

Moreover, the space
Y
can be equipped with the structure of a Riemannian (orbifold) fibration

\displaystyle\vartheta:(Y,g_{Y})\rightarrow(S,g_{S}),

such that
\nu_{0}:(X_{0},g_{0})\rightarrow(S,g_{S})
can be identified with

\displaystyle(\overline{\vartheta}:\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times Y,{\mathrm{d}}r^{2}+g^{2,0}_{Y}+r^{2}g^{0,2}_{Y})\rightarrow(S,g_{S}))

Remark 12
.

Notice that the geometry of the conical fibration is the one of a warped product

\displaystyle(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times Y,{\mathrm{d}}r^{2}+g_{Y;r})

of the real half-line and a collapsing fibration

\displaystyle(Y,g_{Y;r}=g^{2,0}_{Y}+r^{2}g^{0,2}_{Y})\rightarrow(S,g_{S}).

\heartsuit

Let
\epsilon>0
and let

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Tub}}_{3\epsilon}(S):=(Y\times(0,3\epsilon))\subset X_{0}

and

\displaystyle j:{\mathrm{Tub}}_{3\epsilon}(S)\rightarrow U_{3\epsilon}\subset X

be a tubular neighbourhood of width
3\epsilon
, induced by the Riemannian structure.
4
4

4

Later we will assume that
\epsilon\sim t^{\lambda}
for
0\leq\lambda<1
.

Remark 13
.

Usually we will take the exponential tubular neighbourhood

\displaystyle{\mathrm{exp}}_{g}:{\mathrm{Tub}}_{3\epsilon}(S)\rightarrow X

induced by the Riemannian orbifold structure
g
.

\heartsuit

Let
r:X_{0}\rightarrow[0,\infty)
denote the radius function on the normal cone bundle. Then there exists an asymptotic expansion

\displaystyle j^{*}g=g_{0}+g_{j;hot}

and the higher order terms satisfy

\displaystyle|g_{j;hot}|_{g_{0}}=\mathcal{O}(r).

Definition 2.14
.

A tubular neighbourhood
j:{\mathrm{Tub}}_{3\epsilon}(S)\rightarrow X
is called an adiabatic tubular neighbourhood if
g_{j;hot}=0
. We will write
|g_{j;hot}|_{g_{0}}=\mathcal{O}(r^{\sigma})
for
\sigma=1,\infty
.

Given the Riemannian structure
g_{0}
on
X_{0}
, the associated Levi-Civita connection will be denoted by
\nabla^{g_{0}}
. Moreover, there exists an additional metric connection on
X_{0}
given by lift of the Levi-Civita of
g_{S}
along the trivial connection on
VX_{0}
.

Lemma 2.3

(
[
AGR16
]
)
.

The connection
\nabla^{g}
and
\nabla^{\oplus_{0}}=\nabla^{g_{0};HH}\oplus\nabla^{g_{0};VV}
define connections on
\prescript{ie}{}{T[X:X^{sing}]}
and
\prescript{ie}{}{T[X_{0}:S]}
respectively.

This connection will be denoted by
\nabla^{\oplus_{0}}
and its torsion agrees with the curvature of
H_{0}
. This connection can be seen as the diagonal terms of

\displaystyle{\mathrm{res}}_{Y;0}\nabla^{g}=\nabla^{\oplus_{0}}+\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&{\mathrm{II}}^{g}_{S}\\
-({\mathrm{II}}^{g}_{S})^{*}&0\end{array}\right)

We could have define the connection
\nabla^{\oplus_{0}}
via the above expansion.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
(X,g)
be a Riemannian orbifold. Then all the singular strata are (open) totally geodesic and

\displaystyle{\mathrm{res}}_{Y;0}\nabla^{g}=\nabla^{\oplus_{0}}.

Proof.

Notice, that the singular strata locally is given as the fixed-point set of an isometric action of the isotropy group. Hence, by a standard fact from Riemannian geometry, it is totally geodesic.
∎

2.5.
Asymptotically (Quasi) Conical Fibrations

In the following subsection we will introduce the notions of (quasi) conical fibrations ((Q)CF) and asymptotically (quasi) conical fibrations (A(Q)CF). These are models of tubular neighbourhoods of singular strata of orbifolds and their resolutions.

Let in the following
q:(Y,g_{Y})\rightarrow(S,g_{S})
be a (singular) Riemannian fibration
5
5

5

Here singular Riemannian fibration reads (singular Riemannian) fibration and not singular (Riemannian fibration), i.e. the Riemannian structure is singular, not the fibration.
of dimension
m-1+s=n-1
(here
S
is an
s
-dimensional Riemannian manifold) with compact fibres. The Riemannian structure on
Y
induces an Ehresmann connection on the fibration, i.e.
TY\cong H_{Y}\oplus VY
.

Definition 2.15
.

A conical fibration over a link-fibration
(q:(Y,g_{Y})\rightarrow(S,g_{S}))
is given by the (singular) wrapped product

\displaystyle(p:(CF_{S}(Y),g_{CF})\rightarrow(S,g_{S}))=((q:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times Y,{\mathrm{d}}r^{2}+g^{2,0}_{Y}+r^{2}g^{0,2}_{Y})\rightarrow(S,g_{S}))

Usually, we will write
g_{CF}=p^{*}g_{S}+g_{CF;V}
where
g_{CF;V}
denotes the fibrewise conical structure. The connection
H_{Y}
induces the connection
H_{CF}
on
CF_{S}(Y)
via

\displaystyle({\mathrm{id}}\oplus A_{H_{q}}):TCF_{S}(Y)\cong\underline{\mathbb{R}}\oplus TY\rightarrow\underline{\mathbb{R}}\oplus VY.

Given a singular stratum
S
of a Riemannian orbifold, its normal cone bundle naturally carries the structure of a conical fibration. The underlying link-fibration is smooth if the singular stratum is of type (I); otherwise given by an orbifold-fibration over the singular stratum.

Furthermore, given a singular strata
S
of arbitrary type, the link-fibration
Y_{S}
of its normal cone bundle, is a Riemannian orbifold whose singular strata are of “less singular” then the type of
S
.

Definition 2.16
.

A quasi conical fibration (QCF) over a singular link-fibration
(q:(Y,g_{Y})\rightarrow(S,g_{S}))
is given by the (singular) wrapped product

\displaystyle(\nu:QCF_{S}(Y)\rightarrow S,g_{QCF})=(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times\widetilde{Y},g^{2,0}_{Y}+{\mathrm{d}}r^{2}+r^{2}(g_{1/r;Y})^{0,2})

where

\displaystyle(\widetilde{Y},g^{0,2}_{1/r;Y})\xrightarrow[GH]{r\to\infty}(Y,g^{0,2}_{Y})

is a family of (singular) Riemannian metrics converging in the Gromov-Hausdorff sense to the (singular)
(Y,g^{0,2}_{0,Y})
.

In the following we will define the two notions of asymptotically (quasi) conical fibration (A(Q)CF). These spaces are local models of the exceptional set of orbifolds resolutions.

Definition 2.17
.

A Riemannian fibre bundle
\tilde{p}:(X,g_{ACF})\rightarrow(S,g_{S})
is asymptotic to a conical fibration (ACF) over a fibration
(q:(Y,g_{Y})\rightarrow(S,g_{S}))
of rate
\gamma
, if there exists compact subset
B_{R}
and a fibre bundle diffeomorphism
\rho:X\backslash B_{R}\rightarrow CF_{S}(Y)\backslash B_{R}(Y)=(0,R)\times Y
such that

\displaystyle(\nabla^{g_{CF}})^{k}\left(\rho_{*_{ACF}}g_{ACF}-g_{CF}\right)=

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(r^{\gamma})

and
g_{ACF}
is
\tilde{p}
-fibrewise asymptotically conical, i.e.

\displaystyle(\nabla^{g_{CF}}_{V})^{k}\left(\rho_{*_{ACF}}g_{ACF}-g_{CF}\right)=

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(r^{\gamma-k}).

Definition 2.18
.

A Riemannian fibre bundle
\tilde{\nu}:(X,g_{AQCF})\rightarrow S
is asymptotic to a quasi conical fibration (AQCF) over a fibration
(\overline{\nu}:Y\rightarrow S,g_{0;Y})
of rate
\gamma
, if there exists subset
S_{R}
and a fibre bundle diffeomorphism
\rho_{AQCF}:X\backslash S_{R}\rightarrow QCF_{S}(Y)
such that

\displaystyle(\nabla^{g_{QCF}})^{k}\left(\rho_{*_{AQCF}}g-g_{QCF}\right)=\mathcal{O}(r^{\gamma}).

and
g_{AQCF}
is fibrewise quasi conical.

2.6.
Gromov-Hausdorff-Resolutions of Riemannian Orbifolds

In the following subsection we will define Gromov-Hausdorff resolutions of a Riemannian orbifold and describe how these are constructed using geometric resolution data .

Definition 2.19
.

Let
(X,g)
be a Riemannian orbifold. A Gromov-Hausdorff resolution of
(X,g)
is given by a smooth family of (singular) Riemannian orbifolds
(X^{t},g^{t})
and morphisms

\displaystyle\rho^{t}:X^{t}\dashrightarrow X

such that
\rho^{t}
restricts to an orbifold diffeomorphism
(\rho^{t})^{-1}(X^{reg})\cong X^{reg}
, the exceptional set
\Upsilon^{t}=(\rho^{t})^{-1}(X^{sing})
is of codimension
\geq 2
and

\displaystyle(X^{t},g^{t})\xrightarrow[GH]{t\to 0}(X,g)\hskip 28.45274pt\text{and}\hskip 28.45274pt(X^{t}\backslash\Upsilon^{t},g^{t})\xrightarrow[C^{\infty}]{t\to 0}(X^{reg},g)

In particular

\displaystyle{\mathrm{vol}}_{g^{t}}(\Upsilon^{t})\xrightarrow{t\to 0}0\hskip 28.45274pt\text{and}\hskip 28.45274pt\rho^{t}_{*}g^{t}\xrightarrow[C^{\infty}]{t\to 0}g.

A complete Gromov-Hausdorff-resolution of
(X,g)
is given by a smooth family of Riemannian manifolds
(X^{t},g^{t})
resolving
(X,g)
at
X^{sing}
. Gromov-Hausdorff resolutions of Riemannian orbifolds form a category
\widetilde{\textbf{GHRes}}(X,g)
. A Gromov-Hausdorff resolution
(X^{t},g^{t},\rho^{t})
is a
(0,\epsilon)
-family of Riemannian orbifold resolutions. A morphism
(\rho^{t},X^{t},g^{t})\rightsquigarrow(\rho^{\prime t},X^{\prime t},g^{\prime t})
of Riemannian orbifold resolutions is given by a
(0,\min\{\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime}\})
-family of commuting diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(X^{t},g^{t})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(X^{\prime t},g^{\prime t})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(X,g)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho^{t}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi^{t}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho^{\prime t}}

Two resolutions
(\rho^{t},X^{t},g^{t})
and
(\rho^{\prime t},X^{t},g^{\prime t})
are called equivalent, if the morphisms
\phi^{t}
are isometries of Riemannian orbifolds. The category of Gromov-Hausdorff-resolutions of Riemannian, is the quotient category

\displaystyle\textbf{GHRes}(X,g)=\widetilde{\textbf{GHRes}}(X,g)/\sim.

Remark 14
.

When ever talking about a resolution of a Riemannian orbifold, we are usually not interested in its maximal existence family, but rather that the resolution exists in the first place. Hence, the above “germy” definition of the resolution category enables us, to remove a lot of argumentation. Starting form a resolution
(X^{t},g^{t})\in\widetilde{\textbf{GHRes}}(X,g)
certain analytic steps may require, that the
(0,\epsilon)
-family needs to be reduce to an
(0,\epsilon^{\prime})
-family. In the category
\textbf{GHRes}(X,g)
these two remain the same object. By results of
[
PTW17
]
the category
\textbf{GHRes}(X,g)
is nontrivial.

\heartsuit

2.6.1.

In the following section we will describe the data needed to resolve a Riemannian orbifold with a type (I) singular strata of depth 1.

Assumption 1.

(Type (I))
.

Let us assume that there exists a family of ACF spaces
(X_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta},g_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta})\in\textbf{GHRes}(X_{0},g_{0})

2.6.2.
Resolutions of Intersecting Singular Strata

In the following we will discuss resolutions of general Riemannian orbifolds in a toy example in order to avoid an absurd amount of “bookkeeping” data. Nevertheless, the general case can be extrapolated form the following ideas.

Let us assume that the Riemannian orbifold
(X,g)
has three singular strata
S_{1}
,
S_{2}
and

\displaystyle\overline{S_{1}}\cap\overline{S_{2}}=S_{3}
