. We define the metrics

\displaystyle g^{t}_{0_{123}}=

\displaystyle(1-\chi^{t}_{1;3})\chi^{t}_{2;3}\cdot g^{t}_{0_{1}}+(1-\chi^{t}_{1;3})(1-{\chi^{t}_{2;3}})\cdot g^{t}_{0_{3}}+\chi^{t}_{2;3}\cdot g^{t}_{0_{2}}

\displaystyle g^{t}_{\zeta_{123}}=

\displaystyle(1-\chi^{t}_{1;3})\chi^{t}_{2;3}\cdot g^{t}_{\zeta_{1}}+(1-\chi^{t}_{1;3})(1-\chi^{t}_{2;3})\cdot g^{t}_{\zeta_{3}}+\chi^{t}_{2;3}\cdot g^{t}_{\zeta_{2}}

Now let
{\chi^{t}_{3}}
be a smooth, positive function with values in
[0,1]
on
X^{t}
such that

\displaystyle{\chi^{t}_{3}}|_{X^{t}\backslash U^{t}_{3\epsilon}}=1\hskip 28.45274pt{\chi^{t}_{3}}|_{U^{t}_{2\epsilon}}=0

We define a metric

\displaystyle g^{t}=g^{t}_{\zeta_{123}}\cup^{t}g_{0}+\delta^{*}_{t}g_{j;hot}

on
X^{t}
by interpolating between the resolved metric on
B_{5\epsilon}(X_{\zeta_{123}})
and the orbifold metric on
X
.

2.7.
The Cohomology of Resolved Orbifolds

In the following section we will study the topology of the resolution by calculating the cohomology groups in terms of data on the orbifold
X
as well as the ACF space
X_{\zeta}
.

Proposition 2.4
.

There exists a short exact sequence of the cohomology groups

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{H}}^{\bullet}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{H}}^{\bullet}(X^{t})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{H}}^{\bullet}(X_{\zeta})/{\mathrm{H}}^{\bullet}(S)}

and hence

\displaystyle b^{k}(X^{t})=b^{k}(X)+b^{k}(X_{\zeta})-b^{k}(S).

Proof.

We compute the cohomology of
X^{t}
using relative cohomology

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces...}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{H}}^{k-1}({\mathrm{Tub}}_{3t^{-1}\epsilon}(S))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{H}}^{k}(X,{\mathrm{Tub}}_{3\epsilon}(S))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{H}}^{k}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{H}}^{k}({\mathrm{Tub}}_{3t^{-1}\epsilon}(S))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces...}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces...}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{H}}^{k-1}(B_{3t^{-1}\epsilon}(X_{\zeta}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{H}}^{k}(X^{t},B_{3t^{-1}\epsilon}(X_{\zeta}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{H}}^{k}(X^{t})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{H}}^{k}(B_{3t^{-1}\epsilon}(X_{\zeta}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces...}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces...}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{H}}^{k-1}(X_{\zeta})/{\mathrm{H}}^{k-1}({\mathrm{Tub}}_{3t^{-1}\epsilon}(S))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{H}}^{k}(X_{\zeta})/{\mathrm{H}}^{k}({\mathrm{Tub}}_{3t^{-1}\epsilon}(S))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{H}}^{k}(X_{\zeta})/{\mathrm{H}}^{k}({\mathrm{Tub}}_{3t^{-1}\epsilon}(S))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces...}

Now using that
X_{\zeta}
retracts onto
B_{3t^{-1}\epsilon}(X_{\zeta})
and
{\mathrm{Tub}}_{3\epsilon}(S)
onto
S
we conclude the statement.
∎

3.
Hermitian Dirac Bundles on Orbifolds and their Resolutions

In the following section we will study Dirac operators associated to Dirac bundles on orbifolds. Close to the singular strata, we expand these Dirac structures. The leading order term is given by a singular family of Dirac operators on a CF Space. We will then proceed by introducing resolutions of these singular Dirac bundles and construct smooth families of Dirac bundles on the resolved orbifold. This section sets up the right geometric framework for the following sections.

3.1.
Adiabatic Clifford Modules

We will begin this section by briefly discussing families of Hermitian Clifford modules. Let in the following

\displaystyle(W,g^{t}_{W})=(H\oplus V,g_{H}\oplus t^{2}g_{V})

be a
n=m+s
dimensional
\mathbb{R}
-Hilbert space. Let further
{\mathrm{Cl}}(W,g^{t}_{W})
denote its Clifford algebra. Further, let
{\mathrm{R}}\left({\mathrm{Cl}}(W,g^{t})\right)
denote its representation ring and
{\mathrm{Irr}}\left({\mathrm{Cl}}(W,g^{t})\right)
the subset of irreducible representations. We will mostly be interested in the limit
t\to 0
. This is problematic as Clifford multiplication becomes degenerate , i.e for
w\in W
and
\omega\in W^{*}

\displaystyle{\mathrm{cl}}_{g^{t}_{W}}(w^{1,0}\oplus w^{0,1})^{2}=-|w^{1,0}|^{2}_{g_{H}}-t^{2}|w^{0,1}|^{2}_{g_{V}}\hskip 14.22636pt{\mathrm{cl}}_{g^{t}_{W}}(\omega^{1,0}\oplus\omega^{0,1})^{2}=-|\omega^{1,0}|^{2}_{g_{H}}-t^{-2}|\omega^{0,1}|^{2}_{g_{V}}

Remark 15

(
[
BC89
]
)
.

The Clifford algebra of
(W,g^{t}_{W})
is isomorphic to

\displaystyle{\mathrm{Cl}}(H,g_{H})\otimes{\mathrm{Cl}}(V,t^{2}g_{V})\cong{\mathrm{Cl}}(W,g^{t}_{W})

and the representation ring decomposes

\displaystyle{\mathrm{R}}\left({\mathrm{Cl}}(H,g_{H})\oplus{\mathrm{Cl}}(V,t^{2}g_{V})\right)\cong{\mathrm{R}}\left({\mathrm{Cl}}(H,g_{H})\right)\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathrm{R}}\left({\mathrm{Cl}}(V,t^{2}g_{V})\right).

However for irreducible representation this is only true unless both
m
or
s
are odd, then

\displaystyle\rho_{H}\otimes\rho_{V}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{2}

is irreducible for
\rho_{H}\in{\mathrm{Irr}}({\mathrm{Cl}}(H,g_{H}))
and
\rho_{V}\in{\mathrm{Irr}}({\mathrm{Cl}}(V,t^{2}g_{V}))
. However, we will be mostly interested in the case
m
and
s
even.

\heartsuit

Definition 3.1
.

Let in the following
\mathbb{S}
be a
{\mathrm{Cl}}(W,g^{t}_{W})
-module. Then this module decomposes into the following
{\mathrm{Cl}}(H,g_{H})
and
{\mathrm{Cl}}(V,t^{2}g_{V})
-modules, depending on the parity of
n
,
m
and
s
.

n\equiv 0(2)

In this case the representation decomposes into to a positive and a negative
{\mathrm{Cl}}(W,g^{t}_{W})
-representation, i.e.

\displaystyle\mathbb{S}=\mathbb{S}_{+}\oplus\mathbb{S}_{-}

that further decomposes into

m,s\equiv 0(2)

\displaystyle\mathbb{S}_{+}=\mathbb{S}_{+,H}\otimes\mathbb{S}_{+,V}\oplus\mathbb{S}_{-,H}\otimes\mathbb{S}_{-,V}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{and}\hskip 14.22636pt\mathbb{S}_{-}=\mathbb{S}_{+,H}\otimes\mathbb{S}_{-,V}\oplus\mathbb{S}_{-,H}\otimes\mathbb{S}_{+,V}

m,s\equiv 1(2)

\displaystyle\mathbb{S}_{+}=\mathbb{S}_{H}\otimes\mathbb{S}_{V}\otimes\mathbb{R}_{+}\hskip 28.45274pt\text{and}\hskip 28.45274pt\mathbb{S}_{-}=\mathbb{S}_{H}\otimes\mathbb{S}_{V}\otimes\mathbb{R}_{-}

n\equiv 1(2)

In this case the representation does not split, but decomposes accordingly into

m\equiv 0(2)

\displaystyle\mathbb{S}=(\mathbb{S}_{+,H}\oplus\mathbb{S}_{-,H})\otimes\mathbb{S}_{V}

s\equiv 1(2)

\displaystyle\mathbb{S}=\mathbb{S}_{H}\otimes(\mathbb{S}_{+,V}\oplus\mathbb{S}_{-,V})

Let
(\mathbb{S},h)
be a Hermitian
{\mathrm{Cl}}(W,g_{W})
-module. Then
(\mathbb{S},h^{t})
is a Hermitian
{\mathrm{Cl}}(W,g_{H}\oplus t^{2}g_{V})
-module where

\displaystyle(\mathbb{S},h^{t}):=\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}(\mathbb{S}_{n},t^{n}\cdot h|_{\mathbb{S}_{n}})

here the decomposition of
\mathbb{S}
into the
\mathbb{S}_{n}
is
h
-orthogonal. In particular, the Hermitian Clifford modules are isomorphic for all
t
as vector space.

Example 2
.

The most important example for our discussion is the representation of the Clifford algebra on the exterior algebra of
W
, i.e.

\displaystyle(\mathbb{S},h^{t})=(\wedge^{\bullet}W^{*}=\wedge^{\bullet}H^{*}\otimes\wedge^{\bullet}V^{*},g^{t}_{W}).

\clubsuit

Definition 3.2
.

Let
(\mathbb{S},{\mathrm{cl}}_{g^{t}_{W}},h^{t})
be a Hermitian
{\mathrm{Cl}}(W,g^{t}_{W})
-module. We define the space of twisting morphisms

\displaystyle{\mathrm{End}}(\mathbb{S},{\mathrm{cl}}_{g^{t}_{W}},h^{t})=\{T\in{\mathrm{End}}(\mathbb{S}),[{\mathrm{cl}}_{g^{t}_{W}},T]=0\text{ and }h^{t}(T.,.)=h^{t}(.,T.)\}.

Further, there exists a morphism

\displaystyle\rho_{g^{t}}:{\mathrm{End}}(W,g^{t}_{W})\rightarrow{\mathrm{End}}(\mathbb{S},{\mathrm{cl}}_{g^{t}_{W}},h^{t}),\,\rho_{g^{t}}(A)=\tfrac{1}{2}{\mathrm{tr}}([{\mathrm{cl}}_{g^{t}_{W}},{\mathrm{cl}}_{g^{t}_{W}}\circ A]).

This map satisfies

\displaystyle[\rho_{g^{t}}(A),{\mathrm{cl}}_{g^{t}_{W}}(v)]={\mathrm{cl}}_{g^{t}_{W}}(Av).

Definition 3.3
.

Let
q_{g^{t}_{W}}:\wedge^{*}W\rightarrow{\mathrm{Cl}}(W,g^{t}_{W})
denote the canonical quantisation map. We define a morphism
\Lambda^{t}:\wedge^{2}W^{*}\rightarrow{\mathrm{End}}(W^{*})
by

\displaystyle q_{g^{t}_{W}}(\Lambda^{t}(\alpha)\xi)=[q_{g^{t}_{W}}(\alpha),q_{g^{t}_{W}}(\xi)].

Clearly,

\displaystyle\Lambda^{t}(\beta_{1}\wedge\beta_{2})\xi=2(g^{t}_{W}(\beta_{1},\xi)\beta_{2}-g^{t}_{W}(\beta_{2},\xi)\beta_{1}).

The family of maps
\{\Lambda^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
equips
\wedge^{2}W
with a family of Lie-algebra structures via

\displaystyle\Lambda^{t}([\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}]_{t})=[\Lambda^{t}(\alpha_{1}),\Lambda^{t}(\alpha_{2})].

Further, denote let
\overline{\Lambda}^{t}(\alpha)=-(\Lambda^{t}(\alpha))^{\dagger}
. Then

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}g^{t}_{W}(\overline{\Lambda}^{t}(\alpha)X,Y)=\left<\alpha,X\wedge Y\right>

holds.

3.2.
Dirac Bundles on Orbifold

In the following subsection we will recall the definition of Hermitian Dirac bundles on orbifolds and describe the geometry close to the singular stratum. Hermitian Dirac bundles play a fundamental role in differential geometry with prominent examples being the exterior algebra bundle of a Riemannian manifold, Hermitian spinor bundles or the bundles appearing in the deformation theory of gauge theoretic equations such as holonomy instantons.

Definition 3.4
.

Let
\nabla
be a metric connection on
(X,g)
. A Hermitian Dirac bundle on
(X,g)
is given by a tuple

\displaystyle(E,{\mathrm{cl}}_{g},h,\nabla^{h})

consisting of a
{\mathrm{Cl}}(T^{*}X,g)
-module

\displaystyle\pi:E\rightarrow X\hskip 28.45274pt{\mathrm{cl}}_{g}:T^{*}X\rightarrow{\mathrm{End}}(E)

endowed with a Hermitian structure
h
and a Hermitian connection
\nabla^{h}
compatible with the
{\mathrm{Cl}}(T^{*}X,g)
-module, i.e.

\displaystyle\nabla^{h}({\mathrm{cl}}_{g}(\xi)\Phi)=

\displaystyle{\mathrm{cl}}_{g}(\nabla\xi)\Phi+{\mathrm{cl}}_{g}(\xi)\nabla^{h}\Phi

\displaystyle 0=

\displaystyle h({\mathrm{cl}}_{g}(\xi)\Phi,\Phi^{\prime})+h(\Phi,{\mathrm{cl}}_{g}(\xi)\Phi^{\prime})

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}}h(\Phi,\Phi^{\prime})=

\displaystyle h(\nabla^{h}\Phi,\Phi^{\prime})+h(\Phi,\nabla^{h}\Phi^{\prime}).

The Dirac operator associated to the Dirac bundle
(E,{\mathrm{cl}}_{g},h,\nabla)
is given by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D:\Gamma(X,E)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{1}(X,E)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma(X,T^{*}X\otimes E)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma(X,E)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nabla^{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{cl}}_{g}}

If
\nabla\neq\nabla^{g}
we will refer to
\nabla^{h}
as a Clifford module connection with torsion.

Remark 16
.

Notice that since
{\mathrm{cl}}_{g}
is skew-Hermitian

\displaystyle|{\mathrm{cl}}_{g}(\xi)\Phi|^{2}=h({\mathrm{cl}}_{g}(\xi)\Phi,{\mathrm{cl}}_{g}(\xi)\Phi)=g(\xi,\xi)h(\Phi,\Phi)=|\xi|^{2}|\Phi|^{2}.

\heartsuit

Remark 17
.

Expressed in terms as structure on
[X:X^{sing}]
a Hermitian Dirac module structure can be expressed as a Hermitian
{\mathrm{Cl}}(\prescript{ie}{}{T[X:X^{sing}]},g)
-module

\displaystyle\pi:(E,h,\nabla^{h})\rightarrow[X:X^{sing}]\hskip 28.45274pt{\mathrm{cl}}_{g}:\prescript{ie}{}{T[X:X^{sing}]}\rightarrow{\mathrm{End}}(E)

subject to the axioms listed above.

\heartsuit

Remark 18
.

The Clifford multiplication
{\mathrm{cl}}_{g}:T^{*}X\rightarrow{\mathrm{End}}(E)
extends to an algebra morphism

\displaystyle{\mathrm{cl}}_{g}:{\mathrm{Cl}}(T^{*}X,g)\rightarrow{\mathrm{End}}(E).

Given a form
\omega\in\Omega^{\bullet}(X)
. Then
\nabla^{{\mathrm{End}}(E)}{\mathrm{cl}}_{g}(\omega)={\mathrm{cl}}_{g}(\nabla^{g}\omega)
.

\heartsuit

In some case we will require the Dirac bundle to carry an additional geometric structure.

Definition 3.5
.

A grading on a Hermitian Dirac bundle
(E,{\mathrm{cl}}_{g},h,\nabla^{h})
is given by an endomorphism
\epsilon\in\Gamma(X,{\mathrm{End}}(E))
such that

\displaystyle\epsilon^{2}=1,\hskip 28.45274pt[{\mathrm{cl}}_{g},\epsilon]=0,\hskip 28.45274pt\nabla^{h}\epsilon=0\hskip 28.45274pt\text{and}\hskip 28.45274pt\epsilon^{*}h=h.

Given a grading on a Dirac bundle, the Clifford module
E
decomposes into
h
-orthogonal
\pm 1
-eigenspaces

\displaystyle E=E_{+}\oplus E_{-}.

Notice that in this case the Dirac operator decomposes into

\displaystyle D=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}&D^{-}\\
D^{+}&\end{array}\right).

Definition 3.6
.

Define the vector bundle
\pi_{{\mathrm{End}}(E)}:{\mathrm{End}}(E,{\mathrm{cl}}_{g},h)\rightarrow X
of twisting morphisms by

\displaystyle{\mathrm{End}}(E,{\mathrm{cl}}_{g},h)=\{T\in{\mathrm{End}}(E),[{\mathrm{cl}}_{g_{W}},T]=0\text{ and }h(T.,.)=h(.,T.)\}.

Remark 19

(Twisting Connection)
.

Locally
X
is always spin and hence locally the Dirac bundle uniquely decomposes into a twisted spinor module, i.e.

\displaystyle E=_{loc}(SX,b,\overline{\nabla}^{g})\otimes(F,h_{tw.},\nabla^{tw.})

In particular the Hermitian Clifford module connection is the product of a spinor connection and a Hermitian connection on the local twisting bundle. This locally defined connection will be referred to as the twisting connection. Its curvature, is given by a globally defined

\displaystyle F^{tw.}_{\nabla^{h}}\in\Omega^{2}(X,{\mathrm{End}}(E)).

\heartsuit

Lemma 3.1

(Weitzenböck-Formula)
.

The twisting curvature of the Dirac bundle
(E,{\mathrm{cl}}_{g},h,\nabla^{h})
is given by

\displaystyle F^{tw.}_{\nabla^{h}}=F_{\nabla^{h}}-\rho^{g}(R_{g})\in\Omega^{2}(X,{\mathrm{End}}(E,{\mathrm{cl}}_{g},h))

and coincides with the curvature of the locally twisting connection. The twisting curvature contributes to the Weitzenböck-formula

(4)

\displaystyle D^{2}=(\nabla^{h})^{*}\nabla^{h}+\frac{1}{4}{\mathrm{scal}}_{g}+{\mathrm{cl}}_{g}(F^{tw.}_{\nabla^{h}}).

Remark 20

(Bochner Technique)
.

Integrating both hand sides of (
4
) shows that

\displaystyle\left|\left|D\Phi\right|\right|_{L^{2}}^{2}=\left|\left|\nabla^{h}\Phi\right|\right|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\frac{1}{4}\int_{X}{\mathrm{scal}}_{g}|\Phi|^{2}_{h}{\mathrm{vol}}_{g}+\left<\Phi,{\mathrm{cl}}_{g}(F^{tw.}_{\nabla^{h}})\Phi\right>_{L^{2}}.

Notice that the parity of the second an third term of the right hand side heavily influences the existence of solutions. In particular, if mentioned terms are positive, no nontrivial solution to
D\Phi=0
exists.

\heartsuit

3.2.1.
Conically Fibred Hermitian Dirac Bundles on the Normal Cone Bundle

We will now focus on the Dirac bundle and its associated Dirac operator close to the singular stratum.

Let in the following
(X,g)
be a compact, oriented
n
-dimensional Riemannian orbifold and
S
a compact singular stratum of type (I) of codimension
m
.

The Hermitian Dirac bundle
\pi_{E}:(E,{\mathrm{cl}}_{g},h,\nabla^{h})\rightarrow(X,g)
induces a conically fibred (CF) Hermitian Dirac bundle

\displaystyle\pi_{E_{0}}:(E_{0},{\mathrm{cl}}_{g_{0}},h_{0},\nabla^{h_{0}})\rightarrow(X_{0},g_{0}).

Let
\epsilon>0
and let

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{0}|_{{\mathrm{Tub}}_{3\epsilon}(S)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{Tub}}_{3\epsilon}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{E_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}

denote a lift of the tubular neighbourhood. Notice that this lift satisfies

\displaystyle\hat{j}^{*}h=h_{0}+h_{j;hot}

and the higher order terms satisfy

\displaystyle|h_{j;hot}|_{h_{0}}=\mathcal{O}(r).

The only nontrivial part in this construction, is to construct the Hermitian Clifford module connection. Locally, we get an identification

\displaystyle E_{0}=_{loc}SX_{0}\otimes F_{0}

where
F_{0}\rightarrow X_{0}\cong\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times Y
is a translational invariant Hermitian vector bundle with fibrewise flat twisting connection
\nabla^{tw.;0}
.

Hence there exists a Hermitian vector bundle
\pi_{F_{Y}}:(F_{Y},h_{F_{Y}},\nabla^{h_{F_{Y}}})\rightarrow Y
pulling back to
X_{0}
. The connection
\nabla^{h_{0}}
is defined by

\displaystyle\nabla^{h_{0}}=\overline{\nabla}^{g_{0}}\otimes 1+1\otimes\nabla^{tw.;0}.

Using the connection
\nabla^{\oplus_{0}}
we further define the Hermitian connection

\displaystyle\nabla^{\otimes_{0}}=_{loc}\overline{\nabla}^{\oplus_{0}}\otimes 1+1\otimes\nabla^{tw.;0}.

This connection is a Clifford module connection with respect to
\nabla^{\oplus_{0}}
.

Lemma 3.2

(
[
BGV92
]
)
.

The connections
\nabla^{\oplus_{0}}
and
\nabla^{\otimes_{0}}
are related to
\nabla^{g_{0}}
and
\nabla^{h_{0}}
by

\displaystyle\nabla^{g_{0}}=\nabla^{\oplus_{0}}-\frac{1}{2}\overline{\Lambda}(F_{H_{0}})

and

\displaystyle\nabla^{h_{0}}=\nabla^{\otimes_{0}}-\frac{1}{2}{\mathrm{cl}}_{g_{0}}(F_{H_{0}}).

Analogous statements hold for
\nabla^{\oplus_{Y}}
and
\nabla^{\otimes_{Y}}
.

Lemma 3.3
.

The Levi-Civita connection on
(X,g)
expand as

\displaystyle\nabla^{g}=

\displaystyle\nabla^{\oplus_{0}}+\mathcal{O}(r).

Proof.

As
S
is totally geodesic its-second fundamental form vanishes.
∎

Definition 3.7
.

The connection
\nabla^{h}
is called admissible, if the twisting curvature of
\nabla^{\otimes_{0}}
is of type
(2,0)
with respect
H_{0}
and

\displaystyle\nabla^{h}=

\displaystyle\nabla^{\otimes_{0}}+\mathcal{O}(r).

Assumption 1
.

We will assume that
\nabla^{h}
is an admissible Dirac module connection.

As
(E_{0},{\mathrm{cl}}_{g_{0}},h_{0},\nabla^{h_{0}})
is a conically fibred Hermitian Dirac bundle, there exists a Hermitian Dirac bundle

\displaystyle(E_{Y},{\mathrm{cl}}_{g_{Y;r}},h_{Y;r},\nabla^{h_{Y;r}})\rightarrow(Y,g_{Y;r})

over the collapsing family of compact Riemannian fibration
\vartheta:(Y,g_{Y;r})\rightarrow(S,g_{S})
. Similarly we define the
r
-invariant connections

\displaystyle\nabla^{\oplus_{Y}}\hskip 28.45274pt\text{and}\hskip 28.45274pt\nabla^{\otimes_{Y}}.

Remark 21
.

As
(Y,g_{Y;r})
is an associated bundle and the Riemannian structure is induced by the connection
H_{Y}
on
F_{S}
and the standard Riemannian structure on
\mathbb{S}^{m-1}/\Gamma
, the fibres of
\vartheta:(Y,g_{Y;r})\rightarrow(S,g_{S})
are totally geodesic.

\heartsuit

Lemma 3.4

(
[
BGV92
]
)
.

The Dirac operator of the Dirac bundle
(E_{Y},{\mathrm{cl}}_{g_{Y;r}},h_{Y;r},\nabla^{h_{Y;r}})
decomposes into

\displaystyle D_{Y;r}=D_{Y;H;r}+r^{-1}\cdot D_{Y;V}

where
D_{Y;V}
is a vertical
r
-independent Dirac operator. The operator
D_{Y;H;r}
will be referred to as the horizontal Dirac operator. We can identify the horizontal Dirac operator with

\displaystyle D_{Y;H;r}=

\displaystyle{\mathrm{cl}}_{g_{Y}}\circ{\mathrm{pr}}_{H_{Y}}\circ\nabla^{\otimes_{Y}}-\frac{r}{2}{\mathrm{cl}}_{g_{Y}}(F_{H_{Y}})

\displaystyle=:

\displaystyle D^{\otimes_{Y}}_{H}-\frac{r}{2}{\mathrm{cl}}_{g_{Y}}(F_{H_{Y}}).

The Dirac operator with respect to
\nabla^{\otimes_{Y}}
can be identified with

\displaystyle D^{\otimes_{Y}}={\mathrm{cl}}_{g_{Y;r}}\circ\nabla^{\otimes_{Y}}=D^{\otimes_{Y}}_{H}+r^{-1}\cdot D_{Y;V}.

Corollary 3.1
.

The anti commutator

\displaystyle\{D^{\otimes_{Y}}_{H},D^{\otimes_{Y}}_{V}\}=0

vanishes.

Proof.

The fibres of
\vartheta:(Y,g_{Y;r})\rightarrow(S,g_{S})
are totally geodesic. By the construction of
\nabla^{\otimes_{Y}}
and assumption
1
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\{D^{\otimes_{Y}}_{H},D^{\otimes_{Y}}_{V}\}=

\displaystyle{\mathrm{cl}}_{g_{Y};H}\circ{\mathrm{cl}}_{g_{Y};V}\circ[\nabla^{\otimes_{Y}}_{H},\nabla^{\otimes_{Y}}_{V}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.

∎

Corollary 3.2
.

The Dirac operator
D_{0}
associated to
(E_{0},{\mathrm{cl}}_{g_{0}},h_{0},\nabla^{h_{0}})
decomposes into

\displaystyle D_{0}=

\displaystyle{\mathrm{cl}}_{0}\circ\partial_{r}+r^{-1}\frac{m-1}{2}{\mathrm{cl}}_{0}+D_{Y;r}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathrm{cl}}_{0}\circ\partial_{r}+D^{\otimes_{Y}}_{H}-\frac{1}{4}{\mathrm{cl}}_{g_{0}}(F_{H_{0}})+r^{-1}\cdot D_{Y;V}

We will write
D_{0}=D_{0;H}+D_{0;V}

\displaystyle D_{0;H}=D^{\otimes_{Y}}_{H}-\frac{1}{4}{\mathrm{cl}}_{g_{0}}(F_{H_{0}})\hskip 28.45274pt\text{and}\hskip 28.45274ptD_{0;V}={\mathrm{cl}}_{0}\circ\partial_{r}+r^{-1}\cdot\left(D_{Y;V}+\frac{m-1}{2}{\mathrm{cl}}_{0}\right).

Again, the Dirac operator with respect to
\nabla^{\otimes_{0}}
can be identified with

\displaystyle\widehat{D}_{0}={\mathrm{cl}}_{0}\circ\partial_{r}+D^{\otimes_{Y}}_{H}+r^{-1}\cdot\left(D_{Y;V}+\frac{m-1}{2}{\mathrm{cl}}_{0}\right).

Remark 22
.

As we have already established in the section
2.3
Riemannian orbifolds are examples of incomplete edge spaces. Dirac operators hence belong to the class

\displaystyle D\in{\mathrm{Ell}}^{1}_{ie}(E)

of elliptic, first-order, incomplete edge operators as discussed in
[
AGR16
,
AGR23
]
. In particular, the operator
\widehat{D}_{0}
can be identified with the normal operator

\displaystyle\widehat{D}_{0}\leftrightsquigarrow N(D)

and the vertical operator
D_{0;V}
with the indicial operator

\displaystyle D_{0;V}\leftrightsquigarrow I(D).

\heartsuit

Remark 23
.

The Dirac operators
D_{0}
,
D_{Y;r}
,
\widehat{D}_{0}
and
D^{\otimes_{Y;r}}
are self-adjoint.

\heartsuit

Example 3
.

If the Dirac bundle
(E_{0},{\mathrm{cl}}_{g_{0}},h_{0},\nabla^{h_{0}})
is given by

\displaystyle(\wedge^{\bullet}T^{*}X_{0},\epsilon^{g_{0}}-\iota,g_{0},\nabla^{g_{0}})

the corresponding Dirac operator is given by

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}}\oplus{\mathrm{d}}^{*_{g_{0}}}={\mathrm{d}}_{H}\oplus{\mathrm{d}}_{H}^{*_{g_{0}}}+{\mathrm{d}}_{V}\oplus{\mathrm{d}}_{V}^{*_{g_{0;V}}}-\frac{1}{4}{\mathrm{cl}}_{g_{0}}(F_{H_{0}}).

\clubsuit

Lemma 3.5
.

The Dirac operator
D
expands as

\displaystyle D=

\displaystyle\widehat{D}_{0}+\left({\mathrm{cl}}_{g}-{\mathrm{cl}}_{g_{0}}\right)\circ\nabla^{h}+\mathcal{O}(r)

Definition 3.8
.

We define the boundary operator associated to
D_{0}
given by

(5)

\displaystyle\partial_{r}+B:=-{\mathrm{cl}}_{0}\circ\widehat{D}_{0}=

\displaystyle\partial_{r}+D^{\otimes_{Y}}_{H}\circ{\mathrm{cl}}_{0}+r^{-1}\cdot\left(D_{Y;V}\circ{\mathrm{cl}}_{0}+\frac{m-1}{2}\right).

3.3.
Resolutions of Dirac Bundles

The group
(\mathbb{R}_{>0},\cdot)
acts on the normal cone bundle
X_{0}
via dilation

\displaystyle\delta_{t}:X_{0}\rightarrow X_{0},[n_{s}]\mapsto[t\cdot n_{s}].

Notice that by the construction of the Ehresmann connection
H_{0}
, it is invariant under this action. Hence, the Riemannian structure pulls back to

\displaystyle\delta_{t}^{*}g_{0}=g^{t}_{0}=\nu^{*}_{0}g_{S}+t^{2}g_{0;V}.

A lift of the dilation action to the vector bundle
E_{0}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\delta}_{t}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{t}}

induces a family of Hermitian Dirac bundles

\displaystyle(E_{0},{\mathrm{cl}}_{g^{t}_{0}},h^{t}_{0},\nabla^{h^{t}_{0}}).

In particular the module structure is given by

(6)

\displaystyle{\mathrm{cl}}_{g^{t}_{0}}(V)=\hat{\delta}_{t}\circ{\mathrm{cl}}_{g_{0}}((\delta_{t})_{*}V)\circ\hat{\delta}_{t^{-1}}.

and

\displaystyle\pi_{0}:(E_{0},h^{t}_{0})\cong\bigoplus_{i}(E^{i},h^{i}_{0;H}+\hat{\delta}_{t}^{*}h^{i}_{0;V})\rightarrow X_{0}.

Example 4
.

Usually the family of Dirac bundles appears naturally on
X_{0}
. For instance if the Dirac bundle is given by

\displaystyle(E_{0},h^{t}_{0})=(\wedge^{\bullet}T^{*}X_{0},g^{t}_{0})=(\bigoplus_{i}\wedge^{\bullet,i}T^{*}X_{0},t^{-i}g_{0}).

\clubsuit

Remark 24
.

Notice that a grading on
(E_{0},{\mathrm{cl}}_{g_{0}},h_{0},\nabla^{h_{0}})
restricts to a grading on the fibres of
\nu_{0}
.

\heartsuit

Notice that the Dirac operator associated to the dilated Hermitian Dirac bundle satisfies

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma(X_{0},E_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma(X_{0},E_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma(X_{0},E_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma(X_{0},E_{0}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{t}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{D}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{t}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{D}^{t}_{0}}

[

]
.

Assumption 2
.

Let us assume that there exists a family of ACF Hermitian Dirac bundles of rate
\eta
, resolving the family of CF space
(X_{0},g_{0})
and the CF-Hermitian Dirac bundles

that fit into a commuting diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(E_{\zeta},h^{t}_{\zeta})}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{t}}

whereby

\displaystyle(\nabla^{0,1})^{k}((\hat{\rho}_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta})_{*}h_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta;V}-h_{0;V})=

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(r^{\eta-k})

\displaystyle\nabla^{k}((\hat{\rho}_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta})_{*}h_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta;V}-h_{0;V})=

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(r^{\eta}).

Further, we assume that the curvature of
\nabla^{h_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}}

\displaystyle(\nabla^{0,1})^{k}\left((\hat{\rho}_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta})_{*}F^{0,2}_{\nabla^{h_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}}}\right)=

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(r^{\eta-k})

\displaystyle\nabla^{k}\left((\rho_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta})_{*}F_{\nabla^{h_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}}}-F_{\nabla^{h_{0}}}\right)=

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(r^{\eta})

Furthermore, we will also assume that the data is analytic in
t
sufficiently close to
t=0
.

Notice that
E_{\zeta}
are the same underlying vector bundle for all
t
, but the module structure changes.

Assumption 3
.

We will from now on assume that

\displaystyle(E_{\zeta;V},h^{t}_{\zeta;V})=\left(\bigoplus_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}t^{-n}\cdot E_{\zeta;V;n},h_{\zeta;V}\right)

and the that the decomposition is preserved by the connection
\nabla^{h_{\zeta}}
, i.e. it restricts to a Hermitian connection on the Hermitian subbundle
(t^{-n}\cdot E_{\zeta;V;n},h_{\zeta;V})
.

Let in the following denote by

\displaystyle{\mathrm{cl}}_{g^{t}_{\zeta}}:T^{*}X_{\zeta}\rightarrow{\mathrm{End}}(E_{\zeta})

the Clifford multiplication of
T^{*}X
on
E_{\zeta}
. We define the Dirac operator associated to
(E_{\zeta},h^{t},\nabla^{h_{\zeta}},{\mathrm{cl}}_{g^{t}_{\zeta}})
to be the (uniformly) elliptic operator
D^{t}_{\zeta}\in{\mathrm{Diff}}^{1}(E_{\zeta})

\displaystyle D^{t}_{\zeta}={\mathrm{cl}}_{g^{t}_{\zeta}}\circ\nabla^{h^{t}_{\zeta}}

\displaystyle:\Gamma(X_{\zeta},E_{\zeta})\rightarrow\Gamma(X_{\zeta},E_{\zeta}).

As the Dirac bundle
(E_{\zeta},h^{t}_{\zeta},\nabla^{h_{\zeta}})
is ACF with respect to
(E_{0},h^{t}_{0},\nabla^{E_{0}})
the Dirac operator
D^{t}_{\zeta}
is ACF to
D^{t}_{0}
, i.e.

\displaystyle(\nabla^{{\mathrm{Jet}}^{1}E_{0};V})^{k}\left((\rho_{\zeta})_{*}P_{D^{t}_{\zeta;V}}-P_{D^{t}_{0;V}}\right)=

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(r^{\eta-k})

\displaystyle(\nabla^{{\mathrm{Jet}}^{1}E_{0}})^{k}\left((\rho_{\zeta})_{*}P_{D^{t}_{\zeta}}-P_{D^{t}_{0}}\right)=

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(r^{\eta})

where
D^{t}_{\zeta}=P_{D^{t}_{\zeta}}\circ{\mathrm{jet}}^{1}
and
D^{t}_{0}=P_{D^{t}_{0}}\circ{\mathrm{jet}}^{1}
.

Again, using the splitting
H_{\zeta}\oplus VX_{\zeta}
we define the connection

\displaystyle\nabla^{\oplus_{\zeta}}=\nabla^{g^{t}_{\zeta};HH}\oplus\nabla^{g^{t}_{\zeta};VV}.

This connection is
t
-independent and we can write

\displaystyle\nabla^{g^{t}_{\zeta}}=\nabla^{\oplus_{\zeta}}+\frac{1}{2}\overline{\Lambda^{t}_{\zeta}}(\omega_{\zeta}),

where

\displaystyle\omega_{\zeta}(X)(Y,Z)=

\displaystyle g_{\zeta}({\mathrm{II}}_{g_{\zeta}}(X,Z),Y)-g_{\zeta}({\mathrm{II}}_{g_{\zeta}}(X,Y),Z)

\displaystyle+\tfrac{1}{2}\left(g_{\zeta}(F_{H_{\zeta}}(X,Z),Y)-g_{\zeta}(F_{H_{\zeta}}(X,Y),Z)\right.

\displaystyle+\left.g_{\zeta}(F_{H_{\zeta}}(Y,Z),X)\right).

Following
[
BGV92
]
the limit

\displaystyle\nabla^{g^{0}_{\zeta}}=\nabla^{\oplus_{\zeta}}+\frac{1}{2}\overline{\Lambda}^{0}(\omega_{\zeta})

is well-defined. Recall that locally we can write

\nabla^{h^{t}_{\zeta}}=_{loc}\overline{\nabla}^{g^{t}_{\zeta}}\otimes 1+1\otimes\nabla^{tw.,t}
and we define

\displaystyle\nabla^{\otimes_{\zeta}}=\overline{\nabla}^{\oplus_{\zeta}}\otimes 1+1\otimes\nabla^{tw.0}.

Definition 3.9
.

We define the difference torsion

\displaystyle\Omega_{\zeta}=\omega_{\zeta}-\rho_{\zeta}^{*}\omega_{0}

and the twisting one form

\displaystyle\tau^{t}_{\zeta}=\nabla^{tw.t}-\nabla^{tw.0}.

As
g^{t}_{\zeta}
is asymptotically conical fibred with respect to
g^{t}_{0}
we have

\displaystyle\nabla^{k}\Omega_{\zeta}=\mathcal{O}(r^{\gamma})

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\text{and}\hskip 28.45274pt(\nabla^{0,1})^{k}\Omega^{t}_{\zeta}=\mathcal{O}(r^{\gamma-k})

\displaystyle\nabla^{k}\tau^{t}_{\zeta}=\mathcal{O}(r^{\eta})

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\text{and}\hskip 28.45274pt(\nabla^{0,1})^{k}\tau^{t}_{\zeta}=\mathcal{O}(r^{\eta-k})

Remark 25
.

As the resolution datum was assumed to be analytic in
t
,

\displaystyle|\tau^{t}_{\zeta}|=\mathcal{O}(t).

\heartsuit

Definition 3.10
.

We define the connections

(7)

\displaystyle\hat{\nabla}^{g^{t}_{\zeta}}=

\displaystyle\nabla^{\otimes_{\zeta}}+\frac{1}{2}\overline{\Lambda}^{t}_{\zeta}(\Omega_{\zeta})

(8)

\displaystyle\hat{\nabla}^{h^{t}_{\zeta}}=

\displaystyle\nabla^{\otimes_{\zeta}}+\frac{1}{2}{\mathrm{cl}}_{g^{t}_{\zeta}}(\Omega_{\zeta})+\tau^{t}_{\zeta}

(9)

\displaystyle\hat{\nabla}^{\otimes_{\zeta}}=

\displaystyle\nabla^{\otimes_{\zeta}}-\frac{1}{2}k_{\zeta}.

Lemma 3.6
.

The connection
\hat{\nabla}^{g^{t}_{\zeta}}
is metric and
\hat{\nabla}^{\otimes_{\zeta}}
and
\hat{\nabla}^{h^{t}_{\zeta}}
are Hermitian and the latter is a
\left({\mathrm{Cl}}(TX_{\zeta},g^{t}_{\zeta}),\hat{\nabla}^{g^{t}_{\zeta}}\right)
-module connection.

The commutator of
\hat{\nabla}^{h^{t}_{\zeta};V}
and
\hat{\nabla}^{h^{t}_{\zeta};H}
is given by

\displaystyle[\hat{\nabla}^{h^{t}_{\zeta};H},\hat{\nabla}^{h^{t}_{\zeta};V}]=

\displaystyle F_{\hat{\nabla}^{h^{t}_{\zeta}};HV}+\hat{\nabla}^{h^{t}_{\zeta}}_{[H,V]}

Using that
\nabla^{\otimes_{\zeta}}=_{loc}\overline{\nabla}^{\oplus_{\zeta}}\otimes 1+1\otimes\nabla^{tw.,0}
we compute deduce that

\displaystyle[\nabla^{\otimes_{\zeta}}_{X},\nabla^{\otimes_{\zeta}}_{U}]=\nabla^{\otimes_{\zeta}}_{[X,U]}

for all
X\in\mathfrak{X}^{1,0}(X_{\zeta})
and
U\in\mathfrak{X}^{0,1}(X_{\zeta})
. We deduce that

(10)

\displaystyle\left[\hat{\nabla}^{h^{t}_{\zeta}}_{X},\hat{\nabla}^{h^{t}_{\zeta}}_{U}\right]

\displaystyle=\left[\nabla^{\otimes_{\zeta}}_{X},\nabla^{\otimes_{\zeta}}_{U}\right]+\left[\frac{1}{2}{\mathrm{cl}}_{g^{t}_{\zeta}}(\Omega_{\zeta}(X))+\tau_{\zeta}(X),\nabla^{\otimes_{\zeta}}_{U}\right]

\displaystyle+\left[\nabla^{\otimes_{\zeta}}_{X},\frac{1}{2}{\mathrm{cl}}_{g^{t}_{\zeta}}(\Omega_{\zeta}(U))+\tau_{\zeta}(U)\right]+\left[\frac{1}{2}{\mathrm{cl}}_{g^{t}_{\zeta}}(\Omega_{\zeta}(X))+\tau_{\zeta}(X),\frac{1}{2}{\mathrm{cl}}_{g^{t}_{\zeta}}(\Omega_{\zeta}(U))+\tau_{\zeta}(U)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}{\mathrm{cl}}_{g^{t}_{\zeta}}(\nabla^{\oplus_{\zeta}}_{U}\Omega_{\zeta}(X))-\nabla^{\otimes_{\zeta}}_{U}\tau_{\zeta}(X)+\frac{1}{2}{\mathrm{cl}}_{g^{t}_{\zeta}}(\nabla^{\oplus_{\zeta}}_{X}\Omega_{\zeta}(U))+\nabla^{\otimes_{\zeta}}_{X}\tau_{\zeta}(U)

\displaystyle+\left[\frac{1}{2}{\mathrm{cl}}_{g^{t}_{\zeta}}(\Omega_{\zeta}(X))+\tau_{\zeta}(X),\frac{1}{2}{\mathrm{cl}}_{g^{t}_{\zeta}}(\Omega_{\zeta}(U))+\tau_{\zeta}(U)\right].

Following the lines of Lemma
3.4
, the Dirac operator
\widehat{D}^{t}_{\zeta}
on
X_{\zeta}
can be decomposed in the following way.

Lemma 3.7

(
[
BGV92
]
)
.

The Dirac operator decomposes into

\displaystyle\widehat{D}^{t}_{\zeta}=

\displaystyle\widehat{D}^{t}_{\zeta;H}+\widehat{D}^{t}_{\zeta;V}

Here
\widehat{D}^{t}_{\zeta;V}=t^{-1}\cdot D_{\zeta;V}
is a vertical Dirac operator and the horizontal Dirac operator can be identified with

\displaystyle\widehat{D}^{t}_{\zeta;H}={\mathrm{cl}}_{g_{\zeta}}\circ{\mathrm{pr}}_{H_{\zeta}}\circ\hat{\nabla}^{\otimes_{\zeta}}+{\mathrm{cl}}_{g_{\zeta}}(\tau_{\zeta;H}^{t})+t^{-1}\cdot{\mathrm{cl}}_{g_{\zeta}}(\tau^{t}_{\zeta;V})-\frac{t}{4}{\mathrm{cl}}_{g_{\zeta}}(F_{H_{\zeta}}-\rho^{*}_{\zeta}F_{H_{0}}).

Remark 26
.

In terms of the edge calculus, we have replaced the normal operator of
D
by its “resolution”, i.e. an asymptotic operator

\displaystyle\widehat{D}_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}\sim N(D)

as well as an asymptotic operator to the indicial operator

\displaystyle D_{\zeta;V}\sim I(D).

Further, let
\check{r}:X_{\zeta}\rightarrow[0,\infty)

\check{r}(x)=r(\rho_{\zeta}(x))

Definition 3.11

We glue the Hermitian Dirac bundle

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\Gamma}^{t}_{\zeta}:E_{\zeta}|_{B_{3t^{-1}\epsilon}(X_{\zeta})}}

Lemma 3.8
.

The Levi-Civita connection with respect to
g_{t}
satisfies

\displaystyle g_{t}(\nabla^{g_{t}}_{X}Y,Z)=g_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}(\hat{\nabla}^{g_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}}_{X}Y,Z)\cup_{t}g_{0}(\nabla^{\oplus}_{X}Y,Z)+\frac{1}{2}{\mathrm{d}}\chi_{3}\wedge((\rho_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta})_{*}g_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}-g_{0})(X,Y,Z)+\mathcal{O}(r).

The Dirac bundle connection
\nabla^{h_{t}}
satisfies

\displaystyle\nabla^{h_{t}}=\hat{\nabla}^{h_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}}\cup_{t}\nabla^{\otimes_{0}}+\mathcal{O}({\mathrm{d}}\chi_{3}\wedge((\rho_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta})_{*}g_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}-g_{0}))+\mathcal{O}({\mathrm{d}}\chi_{3}\wedge((\hat{\rho}_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta})_{*}h_{t^{2}\cdot\zeta}-h_{0}))+\mathcal{O}(r).

Proof.

Let
X,Y,Z\in\mathfrak{X}(X_{t})
. Then using Koszul’s formula

\displaystyle 2g_{t}(\nabla^{g_{t}}_{X}Y,Z)=Xg_{t}(Y,Z)+Yg_{t}(X,Z)-Zg_{t}(X,Y)+g_{t}([X,Y],Z)-g_{t}([Y,Z],X)+g_{t}([Z,X],Y)

and
\nabla^{g}=\nabla^{\oplus}+\mathcal{O}(r)
. Lifting this locally to the spinor connection and by
\nabla^{h_{t}}
being Hermitian, we deduce the statement.
∎

4.
Analytic setup

In addressing gluing problems associated with Dirac operators, it is essential to define appropriate function spaces for ”matching sections.” Constructing such spaces requires an understanding of the kernel of the boundary operator, as the behaviour of sections near the boundary heavily influences the global solution structure.

The following subsection begins by introducing fundamental concepts in the context of linear gluing problems, focusing on Dirac operators. This process, involves establishing compatibility conditions for sections over the glued manifold and analysing the effects of boundary interactions. We will provide a schematic outline of this process in subsection
4.1
.

Achieving successful linear gluing necessitates selecting function spaces that align with the constraints imposed by the underlying geometry. By carefully constructing these spaces, we establish a foundation for further analysis in nonlinear settings and applications.

4.1.
Linear Gluing

In the following subsection we will schematically describe the linear gluing problem of understanding the kernel and cokernel of the resolved operator
D^{t}
. We will follow the discussion of Taubes and Hutchings
[
HT07
,
HT09
]
to discuss the basic ideas of linear gluing. The following section should be understood as a fairytale version of linear gluing results on orbifold resolutions.

In the following we will be interested in understanding the analytic properties of
D^{t}:V^{k+1}\rightarrow W^{k}
, given two analytic realisations of
\widehat{D}^{t}_{\zeta}:V_{ACF}^{k+1}\rightarrow W_{ACF}^{k}
and
D:V_{CSF}^{k+1}\rightarrow W^{k}_{CSF}
that are Fredholm and an isomorphism of Banach spaces
\widehat{D}_{0}:V_{CF}^{k+1}\rightarrow W_{CF}^{k}
. The analytic facet of this assumption will be the scope of section
5.4
-
8
and will include a detailed discussion of the behaviour of the Dirac operator near the adiabatic limit.

Remark 27
.

For the following discussion
D^{t}
is not required to be essentially self-adjoint.

\heartsuit

Using the gluing morphisms
\Gamma^{t}_{\zeta}
and
\hat{\Gamma}^{t}_{\zeta}
we define

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X_{\zeta},g^{t}_{\zeta})\sqcup(X,g)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X^{t},g^{t})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{X}^{t},\overline{g}^{t})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X_{0},g^{t}_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X_{t},g_{t})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\overline{X}_{t},\overline{g}_{t})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X_{0},g_{0})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigsqcup_{\Gamma^{t}_{\zeta}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigsqcup_{\overline{\Gamma}^{t}_{\zeta}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{t}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{t}}

the spaces
X^{t}
and
\overline{X}^{t}
as well as the Dirac bundles

\displaystyle\pi^{t}:(E^{t},{\mathrm{cl}}_{g^{t}},h^{t},\nabla^{h^{t}})\rightarrow(X^{t},g^{t})\hskip 28.45274pt\text{and}\hskip 28.45274pt\overline{\pi}^{t}:(\overline{E}^{t},{\mathrm{cl}}_{\overline{g}^{t}},\overline{h}^{t},\nabla^{\overline{h}^{t}})\rightarrow(\overline{X}^{t},\overline{g}^{t}).

In the following we are interested in the Dirac equation

(11)

\displaystyle D^{t}(\Psi\cup^{t}\Phi)=0.

Notice, that (
11
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle\Theta^{t}_{ACF}(\Psi\oplus\Phi)\cup^{t}\Theta^{t}_{CSF}(\Psi\oplus\Phi)=0

with

\displaystyle\Theta^{t}_{ACF}(\Psi\oplus\Phi):=\widehat{D}^{t}_{\zeta}\Psi+(D^{t}-\widehat{D}^{t}_{\zeta})(1-\chi^{t}_{5})\Psi+{\mathrm{cl}}_{g^{t}}({\mathrm{d}}\chi^{t}_{3})(\delta_{t}^{*}\Phi-\Psi)

and

\displaystyle\Theta^{t}_{CSF}(\Psi\oplus\Phi):=D\Phi+(D_{t}-D)\chi_{1}\Phi+{\mathrm{cl}}_{g_{t}}({\mathrm{d}}\chi_{3})(\Phi-\delta_{t^{-1}}^{*}\Psi)

where we artificially introduced the new cut-off function
\chi_{1}
and
\chi^{t}_{5}
. In the above expression we suppressed the identifications
\hat{\Gamma}^{t}_{\zeta}
of Dirac bundles. Notice that

\displaystyle\Theta^{t}_{ACF}(\Psi\oplus\Phi)\oplus\Theta^{t}_{CSF}(\Psi\oplus\Phi)\in W^{k}_{ACF}\oplus W^{k}_{CSF}

defines coupled equations on the disjoint union
X_{\zeta}\sqcup X
.

Lemma 4.1

(Meta)
.

Given
\Gamma\in V^{k+1}
and
\Xi\oplus{\mathrm{H}}\in W^{k}_{ACF}\oplus W^{k}_{CSF}
such that

\displaystyle D^{t}\Gamma=\Xi\cup^{t}{\mathrm{H}}

there exists a unique
\Psi\oplus\Phi\in V^{k+1}_{ACF}\oplus V^{k+1}_{CSF}
such that

(12)

\displaystyle\Gamma=\Psi\cup^{t}\Phi\hskip 28.45274pt\text{and}\hskip 28.45274pt\Theta^{t}_{ACF}(\Psi\oplus\Phi)=\Xi\hskip 14.22636pt\text{and}\hskip 14.22636pt\Theta^{t}_{CSF}(\Psi\oplus\Phi)={\mathrm{H}}.

Lemma 4.2

(Meta)
.

Let
\Xi\oplus{\mathrm{H}}\in{\mathrm{ker}}_{ACF}(\widehat{D}^{t}_{\zeta})\oplus{\mathrm{ker}}_{CSF}(D)
. There exists unique

\displaystyle\Psi\oplus\Phi\in\left({\mathrm{ker}}_{ACF}(\widehat{D}^{t}_{\zeta})\oplus{\mathrm{ker}}_{CSF}(D)\right)^{\perp}

such that

(13)

\displaystyle\Theta^{t}_{ACF}((\Xi+\Psi)\oplus(H+\Phi))\oplus\Theta^{t}_{CSF}((\Xi+\Psi)\oplus(H+\Phi))\in{\mathrm{coker}}_{ACF}(\widehat{D}^{t}_{\zeta})\oplus{\mathrm{coker}}_{CSF}(D).

Remark 28
.

A theorem of this form can be proven by realising it as a fixed-point problem. In order to use the contraction mapping principle to solve this gluing problem we need that
\Phi
and
\Psi
satisfy the matching condition

\displaystyle\left|\left|\Phi-\delta_{t^{-1}}^{*}\Psi\right|\right|_{W^{k+1}({\mathrm{A}}_{2\epsilon,3\epsilon}(S))},\left|\left|\delta_{t}^{*}\Phi-\Psi\right|\right|_{V^{k+1}({\mathrm{A}}_{(2t^{-1}\epsilon,3t^{-1}\epsilon)}(S))}\xrightarrow[t\to 0]{}0.

\heartsuit

Definition 4.1
.

We define the approximate kernel and cokernel as the pullbacks

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{xker}(D^{t})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{ker}_{CSF}(D)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{ker}_{ACF}(\widehat{D}^{t}_{\zeta})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma(Y,E_{Y})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{X_{\zeta}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{res}_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{res}_{\mathrm{\partial X_{\zeta}}}}

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{xcoker}(D^{t})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{coker}_{CSF}(D)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathrm{coker}_{ACF}(\widehat{D}^{t}_{\zeta})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Gamma(Y,E_{Y})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

4.2.
Polyhomogeneous Solutions and Eigenbundles

To thoroughly understand the kernel of the boundary operator, it is useful to examine the polyhomogeneous kernel elements that arise in this context. The boundary operator of the Dirac operator
D
, is given by the leading order term of
D_{0}
close to the singular stratum of
X_{0}
. Given that the underlying configuration space (CF) can be expressed as a warped product of
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
with a collapsing fibration
\vartheta:(Y,g_{Y;r})\rightarrow(S,g_{S})
, we are able to employ a global separation of variables approach to study these kernel elements.

This separation of variables provides a powerful framework to analyse the asymptotic behaviour and polyhomogeneous structure of the solutions. Building on methods developed by Ammann and Bär
[
AB98
]
, we will explicitly calculate these polyhomogeneous solutions, leveraging their techniques to handle the boundary conditions imposed on functional analytic realisations of the boundary operator.

In this section, we outline the derivation of these polyhomogeneous kernel elements, detailing how the warped geometry influences the eigenstructure and solution properties of the boundary operator. This analysis is essential for constructing the function spaces needed in subsequent sections and understanding how the polyhomogeneous structure of solutions contributes to the overall gluing framework.

Remark 29
.

The Hilbert spaces
L^{2}_{r}(Y,E_{Y})
are symplectic Hilbert spaces whose symplectic form is given by

\displaystyle\omega_{r}(\Phi,\Psi)=\int_{Y}h_{r}({\mathrm{cl}}_{0}\Phi,\Psi){\mathrm{vol}}_{Y;r}

and
{\mathrm{cl}}_{0}^{2}=-1
the corresponding complex structure. The operator
D_{Y;r}
anticommutes with this complex structure.

\heartsuit

Let
\pi_{Y}:(E_{Y},{\mathrm{cl}}_{g_{Y;r}},h_{Y;r},\nabla^{h_{Y;r}})\rightarrow(Y,g_{Y;r})
over
\vartheta:(Y,g_{Y;r})\rightarrow(S,g_{S})
denote the Hermitian Dirac bundle introduced in section
3.2.1
. We define the infinite-dimensional vector bundle

\displaystyle\vartheta_{*_{L^{2}}}E_{Y}\rightarrow S

of
\vartheta
-fibrewise sections, i.e. for
U\subset S
open

\displaystyle L^{2}(U,\vartheta_{*_{L^{2}}}E_{Y})=L^{2}(\vartheta^{-1}(U),E_{Y})

Notice that this is naturally a
\vartheta_{*_{Cyl}}\underline{\mathbb{R}}
-module. The
{\mathrm{Cl}}(TS,g_{S})
-module structures is given by

\displaystyle{\mathrm{cl}}_{g_{S}}(V)\Phi={\mathrm{cl}}_{g_{Y;r}}(V^{H_{0}})\Phi={\mathrm{cl}}_{g_{Y_{1}}}(V^{H_{0}})\Phi

for vector fields
V\in\mathfrak{X}(S)
and

. Furthermore the connection
\nabla^{\otimes_{0}}
induces a connection on
\vartheta_{*_{L^{2}}}E_{Y}
by

\displaystyle\nabla^{\otimes_{Y}}_{V}\Phi=\nabla^{\otimes_{Y}}_{V^{H_{0}}}\Phi,

whose curvature is given by

\displaystyle F_{\nabla^{\otimes_{Y}}}(U,V)=F_{\nabla^{\otimes_{Y}}}(U^{H_{0}},V^{H_{0}})-\nabla^{\otimes_{Y}}_{F_{H_{0}}(U,V)}.

Naturally the pushforward is equipped with a Hermitian structure

\displaystyle h_{Y;r}(\Phi,\Phi^{\prime})_{s}=\int_{\vartheta^{-1}(s)}\left<\Phi,\Phi^{\prime}\right>_{h_{Y;r}}{\mathrm{vol}}_{r^{2}g_{Y;V}}

given by the fibrewise
L^{2}_{r}
-product. As
\nu_{0}
and
\vartheta
have totally geodesic fibres the connection
\nabla^{\otimes_{Y}}
is Hermitian connection. This connection is a
{\mathrm{Cl}}(TS,g^{S})
-module connection and thus

\displaystyle(\vartheta_{*_{L^{2}}}E_{Y},{\mathrm{cl}}_{g_{S}},h_{Y;r},\nabla^{\otimes_{Y}})

is an infinite dimensional Hermitian Dirac bundle.

4.2.1.
Polyhomogenous Solutions of the Boundary Operator

In the following we will construct polyhomogenous solutions of the boundary operator. This is achieved by solving for

(15)

\displaystyle(\partial_{r}+B)\Phi=0

in
L^{2}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0},L^{2}_{r}(S,\vartheta_{*_{L^{2}}}E_{Y}))
. By previous section, we can use the spectral decomposition and instead solve for

(16)

\displaystyle(\partial_{r}+B_{\Lambda})\Phi_{\Lambda}=0\hskip 28.45274pt\text{and}\hskip 28.45274pt\Phi_{\Lambda}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0},L^{2}_{r}(S,\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}))

Using the
L^{2}
spectral decomposition into
\mathbb{E}_{\Lambda,M_{\Lambda}}
we deduce that (
15
) reads

\displaystyle\left(\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\partial_{r}&\\
&\partial_{r}\end{array}\right)+\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{m-1}{2r}-\frac{\lambda}{r}&\mu_{\Lambda}\\
\mu_{\Lambda}&\frac{m-1}{2r}+\frac{\lambda}{r}\end{array}\right)\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}f_{-}\\
f_{+}\end{array}\right)=0

for
f_{-},f_{+}\in L^{2}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0},\mathbb{E}_{\Lambda,M_{\Lambda}})
. If
\mu_{\Lambda}=0
, this ODE decouples into

\displaystyle\left(\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\partial_{r}&\\
&\partial_{r}\end{array}\right)+\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{m-1}{2r}-\frac{\lambda}{r}&0\\
0&\frac{m-1}{2r}+\frac{\lambda}{r}\end{array}\right)\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}f_{-}\\
f_{+}\end{array}\right)=0

with solutions

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{c}f_{-}\\
f_{+}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}c_{-}\cdot r^{\frac{1-m}{2}+\lambda}\\
c_{+}\cdot r^{\frac{1-m}{2}-\lambda}\end{array}\right).

If
\lambda=0
and hence
\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda}=\mathcal{E}_{0}
, we have

\displaystyle\left(\partial_{r}+\frac{m-1}{2r}+\mu_{0}\right)f=0

which has the solution
f(r)=c\cdot r^{\frac{1-m}{2}}e^{-\mu_{0}r}
. If
\lambda=\pm\frac{m-1}{2}

\displaystyle\left(\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\partial_{r}&\\
&\partial_{r}\end{array}\right)+\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{m-1}{r}&\mu_{\Lambda}\\
\mu_{\Lambda}&0\end{array}\right)\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}f_{-}\\
f_{+}\end{array}\right)=0

then

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{c}f_{-}\\
f_{+}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}C_{1}r^{\gamma_{+}}+C_{2}r^{\gamma_{-}}\\
-\frac{C_{1}\gamma_{+}}{\mu_{\Lambda}}r^{\gamma_{+}-1}-\frac{C_{2}\gamma_{-}}{\mu_{\Lambda}}r^{\gamma_{-}-1}\end{array}\right)

or

\displaystyle\left(\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\partial_{r}&\\

\mu_{\Lambda}&\frac{m-1}{r}\end{array}\right)\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}f_{-}\\
f_{+}\end{array}\right)=0

and

\displaystyle\left(\begin{array}[]{c}f_{-}\\
f_{+}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}-\frac{C_{1}\gamma_{+}}{\mu_{\Lambda}}r^{\gamma_{+}-1}-\frac{C_{2}\gamma_{-}}{\mu_{\Lambda}}r^{\gamma_{-}-1}\\
C_{1}r^{\gamma_{+}}+C_{2}r^{\gamma_{-}}\\
\end{array}\right)

with
\gamma_{\pm}=1-\frac{m}{2}\pm\sqrt{(m/2-1)^{2}+\mu_{\Lambda}^{2}}
. Finally, if
\lambda,\mu_{\Lambda}
are non of the above we decouple this system to

(41)

\displaystyle r^{2}\partial_{r}^{2}f_{-}+(m-1)r\partial_{r}f_{-}-\left(\frac{m-1}{2}-\lambda-\frac{(m-1)^{2}}{4}+\lambda^{2}+\mu_{\Lambda}^{2}r^{2}\right)f_{-}=

\displaystyle 0

(42)

\displaystyle r^{2}\partial_{r}^{2}f_{+}+(m-1)r\partial_{r}f_{+}-\left(\frac{m-1}{2}+\lambda-\frac{(m-1)^{2}}{4}+\lambda^{2}+\mu_{\Lambda}^{2}r^{2}\right)f_{+}=

\displaystyle 0

which has solutions of the form

\displaystyle f_{-}(r)=

\displaystyle r^{1-\frac{m}{2}}{\mathrm{Re}}\left(c_{J}^{-}\cdot J_{\lambda-\frac{1}{2}}(-{\mathrm{i}}\mu_{\Lambda}r)+c_{Y}^{-}\cdot Y_{\lambda-\frac{1}{2}}(-{\mathrm{i}}\mu_{\Lambda}r)\right)

4.2.2.
Right-Inverse of
B_{\Lambda,\mu_{\Lambda}}

In the following we construct a right-inverse by hand and prove a Schauder estimate.

Let
\vec{g}\in C^{\infty}_{cc}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})\otimes\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Then a right-inverse to
B_{\Lambda,\mu_{\Lambda}}
is given by

\displaystyle R_{\Lambda,\mu_{\Lambda}}(\vec{g})(r)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\int_{0}^{r}g_{\mp}(\xi)\frac{f_{\mp}(r)}{f_{\mp}(\xi)}{\mathrm{d}}\xi&\mu\leq 0\\
-\int_{r}^{\infty}g_{\mp}(\xi)\frac{f_{\mp}(r)}{f_{\mp}(\xi)}{\mathrm{d}}\xi&\mu\geq 0\\
\end{array}\right.

where
f_{\mp}(r)
are solutions of the system (
4.2.1
). Notice, that if
R_{\Lambda,\mu_{\Lambda}}(\vec{g})
with
\vec{g}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R})^{2}
exists than it naturally satisfies the boundary condition

\displaystyle R_{\Lambda,\mu_{\Lambda}}(\vec{g})(0)=0

for all
\mu_{\Lambda}\leq 0
.

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
\vec{g}\in C^{k,\alpha}_{\beta-1}(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})^{2}
. The right-inverse
R_{\lambda,\pm\mu_{\Lambda}}
satisfies the Schauder estimate

\displaystyle\left|\left|R_{\lambda,\pm\mu_{\Lambda}}(\vec{g})\right|\right|_{C^{k+1,\alpha}_{\beta}}+\left|\left|\vec{g}\right|\right|_{C^{k,\alpha}_{\beta-1}}\lesssim(1+|\lambda|)^{-1}(1+|\mu_{\Lambda}|)^{-1}\left|\left|\vec{g}\right|\right|_{C^{k,\alpha}_{\beta-1}}

Proof.

Using the asymptotic expansions of the Bessel functions, we conclude that
R_{\lambda,\mu_{\Lambda}}(g)

\displaystyle R_{\lambda,\mu_{\Lambda}}(g)(r)=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{r}g_{\mp}(\xi)\frac{f_{\mp}(r)}{f_{\mp}(\xi)}{\mathrm{d}}\xi

\displaystyle R_{\lambda,\mu_{\Lambda}}(g)(r)=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\epsilon}g_{\mp}(\xi)\frac{f_{\mp}(r)}{f_{\mp}(\xi)}{\mathrm{d}}\xi+\int_{\epsilon}^{r}g_{\mp}(\xi)\frac{f_{\mp}(r)}{f_{\mp}(\xi)}{\mathrm{d}}\xi

\displaystyle R_{\lambda,\mu_{\Lambda}}(g)(r)=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\epsilon}g_{\mp}(\xi)\frac{f_{\mp}(r)}{f_{\mp}(\xi)}{\mathrm{d}}\xi+\int_{\epsilon}^{R}g_{\mp}(\xi)\frac{f_{\mp}(r)}{f_{\mp}(\xi)}{\mathrm{d}}\xi+\int_{R}^{r}g_{\mp}(\xi)\frac{f_{\mp}(r)}{f_{\mp}(\xi)}{\mathrm{d}}\xi

satisfies

\displaystyle|R_{\lambda,\pm\mu_{\Lambda}}(\vec{g})(r)|\sim_{r\to 0}

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{r}\vec{g}(\xi)\left(\frac{r}{\xi}\right)^{1-m/2+\lambda\mp 1/2}{\mathrm{d}}\xi

\displaystyle|R_{-\lambda,\pm\mu_{\Lambda}}(\vec{g})(r)|\sim_{r\to 0}

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{r}\vec{g}(\xi)\left(\frac{r}{\xi}\right)^{1-m/2-\lambda\pm 1/2}{\mathrm{d}}\xi

\displaystyle|R_{\lambda,\mu_{\Lambda}}(\vec{g})(r)|\sim_{r\to\infty}

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{r}\vec{g}(\xi)e^{\pm\mu_{\Lambda}(r-\xi)}{\mathrm{d}}\xi.

Then for small
r
we have

\displaystyle\left|\left|R_{\lambda,\pm\mu_{\Lambda}}(\vec{g})(r)\right|\right|_{C^{0}_{\beta}}\lesssim

\displaystyle\left|\left|\int_{0}^{r}\vec{g}(\xi)\left(\frac{r}{\xi}\right)^{1-m/2+\lambda\mp 1/2}{\mathrm{d}}\xi\right|\right|_{C^{0}_{\beta}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\left|\left|\left|\left|\vec{g}\right|\right|_{C^{0}_{\beta-1}(0,r)}\int_{0}^{r}\left(\frac{r}{\xi}\right)^{1-m/2+\lambda\mp 1/2-\beta}\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\xi}{\xi}\right|\right|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1-m/2+\lambda\mp 1/2-\beta}\left|\left|\left|\left|\vec{g}\right|\right|_{C^{0}_{\beta-1}(0,r)}\left[\left(\frac{r}{\xi}\right)^{1-m/2+\lambda\mp 1/2-\beta}\right]_{0}^{r}\right|\right|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1-m/2+\lambda\mp 1/2-\beta}\left|\left|\vec{g}\right|\right|_{C^{0}_{\beta-1}}.

Using this the
C^{1,\alpha}_{CF;\beta}
-estimate follows from a direct computation and from the fact that

\displaystyle r\partial_{r}R_{\lambda,\pm\mu_{\Lambda}}(\vec{g})=-\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{m-1}{2}-\lambda&\mu r\\
\mu r&\frac{m-1}{2}+\lambda\end{array}\right)\vec{g}+r\cdot\vec{g}.

The higher regularity follows immediately, from differentiating (
4.2.1
) or equivalently (
41
) and (
42
) and we conclude that

\displaystyle\left|\left|R_{\lambda,\pm\mu_{\Lambda}}(\vec{g})\right|\right|_{C^{k+1,\alpha}_{\beta}}\lesssim(1+|\lambda|)^{-1}(1+|\mu_{\lambda}|)^{-1}\left|\left|\vec{g}\right|\right|_{C^{k,\alpha}_{\beta-1}}.

∎

Remark 34
.

As
Y
is compact, both
\lambda
and
\mu_{\Lambda}
are bounded from below.

\heartsuit

4.3.
e- vs. b-Calculus & Hölder vs. Sobolev

In this section, we discuss the challenges and significance of selecting an appropriate functional analytic framework for establishing a uniform elliptic theory for Dirac operators on orbifold resolutions. As outlined in Section
4.1
, the strategy involves decomposing the orbifold resolutions into fundamental components: the orbifold itself and an asymptotically conical fibration resolving the normal cone bundle. These components are interconnected, with the normal cone bundle serving as the anti-gluing space. Consequently, it is essential to construct functional analytic realisations for the operators
D,\widehat{D}^{t}_{\zeta},\widehat{D}_{0}
that ideally allow the realisation of
\widehat{D}_{0}
to be inherited, thereby ensuring that the induced realisations of
\widehat{D}^{t}_{\zeta}
and
D
are Fredholm.

Previous works by Schulze, Mazzeo, and Melrose
[
Sch91
,
Sch98
,
Maz91
,
MV14
,
DM18
]
introduced Sobolev spaces realising extensions of the minimal
L^{2}
-domain of uniformly elliptic iterated edge differential operators, leading to the development of the edge-calculus. However, in the context of Dirac operators on orbifold resolutions, the usage of edge-calculus is appears like
shooting sparrows with canons
.

As shown in the previous section, the kernel of the normal operator admits polyhomogeneous expansions of the form:

\displaystyle\Phi(r)\sim e^{\pm\mu_{\Lambda}r}r^{\pm\lambda+\frac{1-m}{2}+\delta(E_{0})}.

This explicit control over the asymptotic behaviour of kernel and cokernel elements will enable us to define “new function spaces” more suitable for the study of Dirac operators on orbifold resolutions.

The main challenges in defining suitable functional analytic realisations of the Dirac operators
\widehat{D}_{0}
,
D
and
\widehat{D}^{t}_{\zeta}
can be summarised as follows:

1.

Local Control and Rescaling Sensitivity:

Gluing problems require precise local control via
C^{k}
-estimates of pre-glued solutions, which must also be sensitive to rescaling/blow-up arguments.

2.

Infinite-Dimensional Kernel of
\widehat{D}_{0}
:

The normal operator
\widehat{D}_{0}
has an infinite-dimensional kernel comprising solutions to (
41
) and (
42
) for
\lambda,\mu\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Therefore, the choice of function spaces must effectively “exclude” these kernel elements.

3.

Fibred Structure Sensitivity:

Given the asymptotic behaviour:

\displaystyle\nabla^{1,0}\Phi_{\Lambda,M_{\Lambda}}(r)\sim e^{\pm\mu_{\Lambda}r}r^{\pm\lambda+\frac{1-m}{2}+\delta(E_{0})},\quad\nabla^{0,1}\Phi_{\Lambda,M_{\Lambda}}(r)\sim e^{\pm\mu_{\Lambda}r}r^{\pm\lambda-\frac{m+1}{2}+\delta(E_{0})}(1+r^{-1}),

the function spaces must be adapted to the fibred structures of
X_{0}
and
X_{\zeta}
to facilitate a meaningful adiabatic limit analysis.

4.

Unbounded Operators on Weighted Spaces:

For functions
|\Phi|\sim r^{\beta}
, we have:

\displaystyle\widehat{D}_{0}\Phi\sim r^{\beta}+r^{\beta-1}.

This behaviour implies that the analytic realisations act as unbounded operators on the corresponding weighted function spaces.

To address these challenges, we propose the following strategy:

\Rightarrow
1.

Hölder vs. Sobolev Spaces:

Due to the need for fine local control, we will use local Hölder norms.

\Rightarrow
2.

Weighted Function Spaces and Boundary Conditions:

We define weighted function spaces based on solutions to
\widehat{D}_{0}
and impose boundary conditions to restrict the infinite-dimensional kernel. Both the choice of weights and the boundary conditions will ensure that a “half-space” of kernel elements of
\widehat{D}_{0}
are excluded. Combing both ensures that the space of kernel elements of
\widehat{D}_{0}
is finite dimensional. Specifically, we focus on weights derived from special solutions of (
41
) and (
42
) for
\mu=0
, corresponding to fibrewise conical weight functions. The remaining kernel will be controlled by imposing APS-type boundary conditions.

\Rightarrow
3.

Transition from e- to b-Norms:

Instead of edge-norms, we adopt b-norms ensuring that smooth homogeneous elements are appropriately incorporated. Hence, only vertical derivative are weighted by an increased weight.

\Rightarrow
4.

Weighted Hölder to Graph Norms:

The weighted Hölder norms are replaced by graph norms to guarantee that the operators
D,\widehat{D}^{t}_{\zeta},\widehat{D}_{0}
are left semi-Fredholm.

5.
Uniform Elliptic Theory for Conically Fibred Dirac Operators

In this first subsection we will introduce weighted function spaces on conically fibration (CF). These are a certain subclass of quasi conically spaces and allow finer weighted analysis then the latter class. In particular, the normal cone bundles of singular strata of orbifolds are such CF spaces and they are the model at infinity of both asymptotically conically fibred (ACF) spaces and conically singular fibred (CSF) spaces.

Furthermore, compactly supported deformations of CF spaces can be identified with the anti-gluing spaces
\overline{X}^{t}
and hence, studying their geometry is essential for the following sections.

Using some Weitzenböck type argument we will be able to show improved Schauder-estimates for CF Dirac operators and prove that these operators are injective on the chosen CF-weighted Hölder space realisations.

5.1.
Weighted Function Spaces on Conically Fibrations

Let in the following
q:(Y,g_{Y})\rightarrow(S,g_{S})
be a fibre bundle and a Riemannian submersion of compact Riemannian manifolds. Further, the Riemannian structure on
Y
induces an Ehresmann connection on the fibration, i.e.
TY\cong H_{Y}\oplus VY
.

Definition 5.1
.

A conical fibration over
(q:(Y,g_{Y})\rightarrow(S,g_{S}))
of dimension
m-1+s=n-1
(here
S
is an
s
-dimensional compact manifold) is given by the Riemannian manifold

\displaystyle(p:(CF_{S}(Y),g_{CF})\rightarrow(S,g_{S}))=((q:\mathbb{R}_{>0}\times Y,{\mathrm{d}}r^{2}+g^{2,0}_{Y}+r^{2}g^{0,2}_{Y})\rightarrow(S,g_{S}))

Usually, we will write
g_{CF}=p^{*}g_{S}+g_{CF;V}
where
g_{CF;V}
denotes the fibrewise conical structure. The connection
H_{CF}
on
CF_{S}(Y)
is induced by
H_{Y}
by

\displaystyle({\mathrm{id}}\oplus A_{H_{q}}):TCF_{S}(Y)\cong\underline{\mathbb{R}}\oplus TY\rightarrow\underline{\mathbb{R}}\oplus VY.

Remark 35
.

Notice that the geometry of the conical fibration is the one of a warped product

\displaystyle(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\times Y,{\mathrm{d}}r^{2}+g_{Y;r})

of the real line and a collapsing fibration

\displaystyle(Y,g_{Y;r})\rightarrow(S,g_{S}).

\heartsuit

Notation
.

We will denote by
w_{CF}=r:CF_{S}(Y)\rightarrow[0,\infty)
and set

\displaystyle w^{-\beta}_{CF}=w_{CF;\beta}\hskip 28.45274pt\text{and}\hskip 28.45274ptw_{CF}(x,y)={\mathrm{min}}(w_{CF}(x),w_{CF}(y)).

where
\beta\in C^{\infty}(S)
.

Notation
.

In the following we will write
B_{R}(S)=r^{-1}([0,R))
and
A_{R,R^{\prime}}(S)=r^{-1}((0,R,R^{\prime}))
.

Definition 5.2
.

A Hermitian vector bundle
(E_{CF},h_{CF})
is called homogeneous CF Hermitian of degree
\delta(E_{CF})
, if the pullback under the automorphism

\displaystyle w^{Cyl}_{CF;\delta(E_{CF})}:E_{CF}\rightarrow E_{CF},v_{x}\mapsto r^{\delta(E_{CF})}v_{x}

i.e.

\displaystyle(E_{CF},(w^{Cyl}_{CF;-\delta(E_{CF})})^{*}h_{CF})

is a translational invariant Hermitian bundle. A Hermitian vector bundle
(E_{CF},h_{CF})
is called CF Hermitian if it decomposes into the direct sum of homogeneous CF Hermitian bundles. In the following we will write
\delta(E_{CF})
for the tuple of homogeneous degrees and

\displaystyle w^{Cyl}_{CF;\delta(E_{CF})}:E_{CF}\rightarrow E_{CF}

for the induced automorphisms.

Example 5
.

The tangent bundle
(TCF_{S}(Y),g_{CF})
has degree
(0,-1)
. If we refer to degree of
TCF_{S}(Y)
we will always assume the former except mentioned otherwise.

\clubsuit

Definition 5.3
.

Let
(E_{CF},h_{CF})
be a CF Hermitian vector bundle. A Hermitian connection
(\nabla^{h_{CF}})
is called CF Hermitian connection, if

\displaystyle(w^{Cyl}_{CF;d_{T^{*}CF_{S}(Y)\otimes E_{CF}}})_{*}\nabla^{h_{CF}}(w^{Cyl}_{CF;\delta(E_{CF})})^{*}

Is translational invariant.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
\nabla_{CF}
be a Hermitian CF connection. In the presence of the Ehresmann connection
H
the connection
\nabla_{CF}
decomposes into

\displaystyle\nabla_{CF}=\nabla^{H}_{CF}+\nabla^{V}_{CF}

where
\nabla^{V}_{CF}
is a vertical connection, i.e. it restricts
p
-fibrewise to a connection on
E
.

Notation
.

Let
{\mathrm{word}}^{+}_{i,j}(A,B)
denote the sum of all words of length
j
in
A
and
B
counting
i
-times the letter
A
. In particular
{\mathrm{word}}^{+}_{i,j}(A,B)={\mathrm{word}}^{+}_{j-i,j}(B,A)
.

Let
(E_{0},h_{0},\nabla_{0})
a CF-bundle over the CF-space
(X_{0},g_{0})
. Here the Hermitian connection
\nabla_{0}
is either the given by the Clifford module connection
(\nabla^{h_{0}},\nabla^{g_{0}})
or
(\nabla^{\otimes_{0}},\nabla^{\oplus_{0}})
.

Definition 5.4
.

We define the space
\Gamma^{k}_{CF;\beta}(X_{0},E_{0},\nabla_{0})
to be the space of all
C^{k}
-sections
\Phi
such that

\displaystyle\left|\left|w_{CF;\beta}\left\{\nabla^{j}_{0}\right\}\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{0}}<\infty

where

\displaystyle w_{CF;\beta}\left\{\nabla^{j}_{0}\right\}:=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{j}w_{CF;(\beta-j+i)}{\mathrm{word}}^{+}_{i,j}(\nabla^{1,0}_{0},\nabla^{0,1}_{0})

and equip the spaces
\Gamma^{k}_{CF;\beta}(X_{0},E_{0},\nabla_{0})
with the metric

\displaystyle\left|\left|\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{k}_{CF;\beta}}=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{k}\left|\left|w_{CF;\beta}\left\{\nabla^{j}_{0}\right\}\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{0}_{g_{0},h_{0}}}

The space
\Gamma_{CF;\beta}(X_{0},E_{0},\nabla_{0})
is defined to be the intersection of all
\Gamma^{k}_{CF;\beta}(X_{0},E_{0},\nabla_{0})
for all
k
. Furthermore, we define the
w_{CF;\beta}
-weighted Sobolev
W^{k,p}_{CF;\beta}(X_{0},E_{0},\nabla_{0})
and Hölder spaces
C^{k,\alpha}_{CF;\beta}(X_{0},E_{0},\nabla_{0})
by replacing the usual metrics by the ones

\displaystyle\left|\left|\Phi\right|\right|^{p}_{W^{k,p}_{CF;\beta}}=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{k}\int_{X}|w_{CF;\beta}\left\{\nabla^{i}_{0}\right\}\Phi|^{p}_{h_{0},g_{0}}w_{CF;m}{\mathrm{vol}}_{g_{0}}

\displaystyle\left|\left|\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{k,\alpha}_{CF;\beta}}=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{k}\left|\left|w_{CF;\beta}\left\{\nabla^{j}_{0}\right\}\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{0}_{g_{0},h_{0}}}+[\nabla^{j}_{0}\Phi]_{C^{0,\alpha}_{\beta-j;CF}}

\displaystyle[\nabla^{j}_{0}\Phi]_{C^{0,\alpha}_{\beta-j;CF}}=

\displaystyle\underset{B_{g_{0}}}{{\mathrm{sup}}}\left\{\bigoplus_{i=0}^{j}w_{CF;(\beta-j+i-\alpha)}(x,y)\cdot\right.

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\left|{\mathrm{word}}^{+}_{i,j}(\nabla^{H},\nabla^{V})\Phi(x)-\Pi^{x,y}{\mathrm{word}}^{+}_{i,j}(\nabla^{H},\nabla^{V})\Phi(y)\right|_{h,g}}{d_{g}(x,y)^{\alpha}}\right\}

where
B_{g_{0}}(U)=\{x,y\in U|\,x\neq y,\,{\mathrm{dist}}_{g_{0}}(x,y)\leq w_{CF;(\beta-j+i-\alpha)}(x,y)\}\subset U\times U
and
\Pi^{x,y}
denotes the parallel transport along the geodesic connecting
x
and
y
.

Remark 36
.

All
W^{k,p}_{CF;\beta}
,
\Gamma^{k}_{CF;\beta}
and
C^{l,\alpha}_{CF;\beta}
are Banach spaces,
H^{k}_{CF;\beta}=W^{k,2}_{CF;\beta}
is an Hilbert space, but
\Gamma_{CF;\beta}
is not an Banach space.

\heartsuit

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1
. The
L^{2}
-product induces a perfect paring

\displaystyle L^{p}_{CF;\beta}\cong(L^{q}_{CF;-\beta-m})^{*}.

Recall that there exists a
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
-action on
X_{0}
by dilation of the fibres. By assumption, this action can be lifted to an action on
E_{0}
inducing a family of Hermitian Dirac bundles

\displaystyle(E_{0},{\mathrm{cl}}_{g^{t}_{0}},h^{t}_{0},\nabla^{h^{t}_{0}}).

In the following we will show that the elliptic estimates established in section
5.3
can be improved to uniform estimates for this family.

Definition 5.5
.

Given a weight function
w_{0}
for
(X_{0},g_{0})
we define new weight functions on
(X_{0},g^{t}_{0})
by

\displaystyle w_{CF;t}:=\delta_{t}^{*}w_{0}=t\cdot w_{0}=t\cdot w_{CF}.

5.2.
The Restriction and Extension Map

In the following we will impose boundary conditions on the function spaces realising the boundary operator. This should be seen as an analogy to APS boundary condition in the case of Dirac operators on manifolds with boundaries (i.e.
m=0
). These boundary conditions ensure that the operator
\widehat{D}_{0}
can be realised as an isomorphism, which is crucial in linear gluing.

In section
4.2.1
we have solved
\widehat{D}_{0}\Phi=0
using the spectral decomposition of
L^{2}(Y,E_{Y})
into

\displaystyle L^{2}(Y,E_{Y})=\overline{\bigoplus_{|\lambda|\in|\sigma(D_{Y})|}L^{2}(S,\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda})}=\overline{\bigoplus_{|\lambda|\in|\sigma(D_{Y})|}\bigoplus_{\mu_{\Lambda}\in\sigma(B_{\Lambda;H})}\mathbb{E}_{\Lambda,\mu_{\Lambda}}}.

Definition 5.6
.

We define the restriction map

\displaystyle{\mathrm{res}}^{\varepsilon}_{CF}:C^{k,\alpha}_{loc}(X_{0},E_{0},\nabla^{\otimes_{0}})\rightarrow C_{\varepsilon}^{k,\alpha}(Y,E_{Y},\nabla^{\otimes_{Y}})

\displaystyle\Phi\mapsto\Phi|_{r=\varepsilon}

and its section

\displaystyle{\mathrm{ext}}^{\varepsilon}_{CF}:C^{k,\alpha}_{\varepsilon}(Y,E_{Y},\nabla^{\otimes_{Y}})\rightarrow C^{k,\alpha}_{loc}(X_{0},E_{0},\nabla^{\otimes_{0}})

\displaystyle\Phi_{\lambda,\mu}\mapsto f_{\lambda,\mu}(r)\Phi_{\lambda,\mu}.

where
f_{\lambda,\mu}
as the appropriate solution to
4.2.1
.

Obviously, the extension map satisfies

\displaystyle\widehat{D}_{0}{\mathrm{ext}}^{\varepsilon}\Phi=0.

Notice that demanding the extension map to be valued in
C^{k,\alpha}_{CF;\beta}(X_{0},E_{0})
, corresponds to restricting it to the
C^{k,\alpha}
-completion of the set

\displaystyle F_{\beta}=\left\{\left.\sum_{fin.}\Phi_{\lambda,\mu_{\Lambda}}\right|\lambda+\frac{m-1}{2}-\delta(E_{0})>\beta,\,\mu_{\Lambda}\leq 0\right\}.

The boundary conditions will be imposed by demanding the restriction map to be valued in specific subspace of the boundary Hölder space.

Definition 5.7
.

Let

\displaystyle F_{\geq 0}=\left\{\left.\sum_{fin.}\Phi_{\lambda,\mu_{\Lambda}}\right|\mu_{\Lambda}\geq 0\right\}\subset\bigoplus_{|\lambda|\in|\sigma(D_{Y})|}L^{2}_{[0,\infty)}(S,\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda})

be the sum of finite Fourier modes of positive
B_{H;\Lambda}
-eigenvalues, i.e.
L^{2}_{[0,\infty)}(S,\mathcal{E}_{\Lambda})
is the positive spectrum of the operators
B_{\Lambda;H}
. We define the space

\displaystyle C^{k,\alpha}(Y,E_{Y},\nabla^{\otimes_{Y}};+)=\overline{F_{\geq 0}}^{||.||_{C^{k,\alpha}}}

as the completion of
F_{+}
. Define the Banach subspace

\displaystyle C^{k+1,\alpha}_{CF;\beta}(X_{0},E_{0},\nabla^{\otimes_{0}};+)=C^{k+1,\alpha}_{CF;\beta}(X_{0},E_{0},\nabla^{\otimes_{0}})\cap({\mathrm{res}}^{\varepsilon})^{-1}C^{k+1,\alpha}_{r}(Y,E_{Y},\nabla^{\otimes_{Y}};+).

Lemma 5.3
.

Different choices of
\varepsilon
in the above definition yield equivalent
C^{k+1,\alpha}_{CF;\beta}(X_{0},E_{0},\nabla^{\otimes_{0}};+)
.

Proof.

The scaled dilation
\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{\varepsilon^{\prime}}\right)^{\delta(E_{0})}\delta_{\frac{\varepsilon^{\prime}}{\varepsilon}}
defines an isometry of Banach spaces.
∎

Remark 37
.

In particular, the intersection of the preimage of the extension and the image of the restriction map

\displaystyle({\mathrm{ext}}^{\varepsilon})^{-1}\left(C^{k,\alpha}_{CF;\beta}\right)\cap C^{k,\alpha}(Y,E_{Y},\nabla^{\otimes_{Y}};+)=\left\{\Phi_{\beta-\frac{m-1}{2}+\delta(E_{0}),0}\right\}

is empty unless
\beta\in\mathcal{C}(\widehat{D}_{0})

\heartsuit

5.3.
Invertibility of Conically Fibred Dirac Operators

In the following we will develop a uniform elliptic theory for the operator
\widehat{D}_{0}
on the CF Dirac bundle
(E_{0},{\mathrm{cl}}_{g_{0}},h_{0},\nabla^{\otimes_{0}})
.

Definition 5.8
.

We define the set of critical rates of
\widehat{D}_{0}
,
D
and
\widehat{D}^{t}_{\zeta}
to be

\displaystyle\lambda\in\mathcal{C}(\widehat{D}_{0})=\left\{\lambda+\frac{m-1}{2}-\delta(E_{0})\in\sigma(D_{Y;V})\right\}.

Remark 38
.

In the appendix
B
we discuss the spectra of Dirac operators on homology spheres, to determine the set
\mathcal{C}(\widehat{D}_{0})
of critical rates.

\heartsuit

Lemma 5.4
.

The analytic realisation of the family of operators
\widehat{D}_{0}
seen as a map

(45)

\displaystyle\widehat{D}_{0}:

\displaystyle{\mathrm{dom}}_{C^{k+1,\alpha}_{CF;\beta}}(\widehat{D}_{0})\subset C^{k+1,\alpha}_{CF;\beta}(X_{0},E_{0},\nabla^{\otimes_{0}})\rightarrow C^{k,\alpha}_{CF;\beta-1}(X_{0},E_{0},\nabla^{\otimes_{0}})

is unbounded map of Banach spaces.

Lemma 5.5

(Technical Lemma)
.

Let
\chi_{i}
be defined as in section
2.6
. Then

\displaystyle\left|\left|\chi_{i}\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{k,\alpha}_{CF;\beta+\omega}}\lesssim

\displaystyle\epsilon^{-\omega}\left|\left|\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{k,\alpha}_{CF;\beta}(X_{0}\backslash{\mathrm{Tub}}_{(i-1)\epsilon}(S))}

\displaystyle\left|\left|(1-\chi_{i})\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{k,\alpha}_{CF;\beta+\omega}}\lesssim

\displaystyle(1+\epsilon^{-\omega})\left|\left|\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{k,\alpha}_{CF;\beta}({\mathrm{Tub}}_{i\epsilon}(S))}

\displaystyle\left|\left|{\mathrm{cl}}_{g_{0}}({\mathrm{d}}\chi_{i})\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{k,\alpha}_{CF;\beta+\omega}}\lesssim

\displaystyle\epsilon^{-1-\omega}\left|\left|\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{k,\alpha}_{CF;\beta}(A_{(i-1)\epsilon,i\epsilon}(S))}.

Proof.

Notice that
\nabla^{\oplus_{0}}\chi_{i}\Phi=\chi_{i}\nabla^{\oplus_{0}}\Phi+(\partial_{r}\chi_{i}){\mathrm{d}}r\otimes\Phi
and

\displaystyle\left|\left|\partial_{r}^{j}{\chi_{i}}\right|\right|_{C^{0,\alpha}_{\omega-j}}=

\displaystyle\sup_{r\in[\epsilon(i-1),\epsilon i]}r^{j-\omega}\epsilon^{-j}\left|(\partial_{r}^{j}{\chi})(\epsilon^{-1}r-i)\right|

\displaystyle+\sup_{|x-y|\in[0,\epsilon]}{\mathrm{min}}\{x,y\}^{-\omega+j+\alpha}\epsilon^{-j}\frac{\left|(\partial_{r}^{j}{\chi})(\epsilon^{-1}x-i)-(\partial_{r}^{j}{\chi})(\epsilon^{-1}y-i)\right|}{|x-y|^{\alpha}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\epsilon^{-\omega}\left|\left|\partial^{j}_{r}\chi\right|\right|_{C^{0,\alpha}}

to deduce the statements.
∎

We need to point out two key observation. The first one is manifested in the following lemma.

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
\Phi\in C^{k,\alpha}_{loc}(X_{0},E_{0})
. Then

(46)

\displaystyle\left|\left|\widehat{D}_{0}(1-\chi_{i})\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{k,\alpha}_{CF;\beta-1}}\lesssim

\displaystyle\left|\left|(1-\chi_{i})\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{k+1,\alpha}_{CF;\beta}}

(47)

\displaystyle\left|\left|\widehat{D}_{0}\chi_{i}\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{k,\alpha}_{CF;\beta-1}}\lesssim

\displaystyle\left|\left|\chi_{i}\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{k+1,\alpha}_{CF;\beta-1}}.

In particular if we define

\displaystyle C^{k+1,\alpha}_{\beta;\delta}=\left\{\Phi\left|(1-\chi_{i})\Phi\in C^{k+1,\alpha}_{CF;\beta},\chi_{i}\Phi\in C^{k+1,\alpha}_{CF;-\delta}\right.\right\}

as in
[
Maz91
]
, then

\displaystyle\widehat{D}_{0}:C^{k+1,\alpha}_{\beta;1-\beta}\rightarrow C^{k,\alpha}_{\beta-1;1-\beta}.

is a bounded map of Banach spaces.

The second observation goes as follows. Let us assume that we are in a conical setting. By a prior comment we know that all solutions
\widehat{D}_{0}\Phi=0
are homogeneous
\sim r^{\lambda}
. So then if

\displaystyle\chi_{i}\cdot\Phi\in\Gamma_{\beta}(X_{0},E_{0})\,\text{if }\lambda<\beta\hskip 28.45274pt\text{and}\hskip 28.45274pt(1-\chi_{i})\cdot\Phi\in\Gamma_{\beta}(X_{0},E_{0})\,\text{if }\beta<\lambda

but

\displaystyle(1-\chi_{i})\cdot\Phi\notin\Gamma_{\beta}(X_{0},E_{0})\,\text{if }\lambda<\beta\hskip 28.45274pt\text{and}\hskip 28.45274pt\chi_{i}\cdot\Phi\notin\Gamma_{\beta}(X_{0},E_{0})\,\text{if }\beta<\lambda.

In the conically fibred setup this no longer holds, as solutions to
\widehat{D}_{0}\Phi=0
are homogeneous of
\Phi\sim r^{\lambda}e^{\mu r}
. Hence, imposing a polynomial decay only “kills” half of the infinite dimensional kernel. As imposing conical weights implies that
\mu\leq 0
.

Definition 5.9
.

We will denote the domain of
\widehat{D}_{0}
by

\displaystyle C^{k+1,\alpha}_{CF;\beta}(\widehat{D}_{0};+)=\left\{\left.\Phi\in C^{k+1,\alpha}_{CF;\beta}(X_{0},E_{0},\nabla^{\otimes_{0}};+)\right|\widehat{D}_{0}\Phi\in C^{k,\alpha}_{CF;\beta-1}(X_{0},E_{0},\nabla^{\otimes_{0}})\right\}

and equipped it with the graph norm

\displaystyle\left|\left|\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{k+1,\alpha}_{CF;\beta}(\widehat{D}_{0})}=\left|\left|\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{k+1,\alpha}_{CF;\beta}(\nabla^{\otimes_{0}})}+\left|\left|\widehat{D}_{0}\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{k,\alpha}_{CF;\beta-1}(\nabla^{\otimes_{0}})}.

Lemma 5.7
.

The space
C^{k+1,\alpha}_{CF;\beta}(\widehat{D}_{0};+)
is a Banach space and for
\delta>\beta
there exists a compact embedding

(48)

\displaystyle C^{k+1,\alpha}_{CF;\beta}(\widehat{D}_{0};+)\hookrightarrow C^{0}_{CF;\delta}(X_{0},E_{0}).

Proof.

By the Hölder embedding we have an compact embedding

\displaystyle C^{k,\alpha}_{CF;\beta}\hookrightarrow C^{0}_{CF;\delta}.

As
C^{k+1,\alpha}_{CF;\beta}(\widehat{D}_{0};+)\hookrightarrow C^{k+1,\alpha}_{CF;\beta}\times C^{k,\alpha}_{CF;\beta-1}
is a sub-Banach space and

\displaystyle C^{k+1,\alpha}_{CF;\beta}\times C^{k,\alpha}_{CF;\beta-1}\hookrightarrow C^{0}_{CF;\delta}\times C^{0}_{CF;\delta}\twoheadrightarrow C^{0}_{CF;\delta}

is an compact embedding. Hence, we conclude the statement.
∎

Proposition 5.1

(CF-Schauder Estimates)
.

Let
\Phi\in C^{k+1,\alpha}_{CF;\beta}(\widehat{D}_{0};+)
, then

(49)

\displaystyle\left|\left|\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{k+1,\alpha}_{CF;\beta}(\widehat{D}_{0})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left|\left|\widehat{D}_{0}\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{k,\alpha}_{CF;\beta-1}}+\left|\left|\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{0}_{CF;\beta}}.

Proof.

As Schauder estimates are local in the sense that proving the statement on a cover of
X_{0}
suffices to proof the statement. We start with the statement for
k=0
.

Let
\mathcal{U}_{\varepsilon}
denote a good cover of
S
by geodesic balls of radius
\varepsilon
and let
\nu_{0}^{-1}\mathcal{U}_{\varepsilon}
denote the cover of
{\mathrm{Tub}}_{2\epsilon}(S)
by opens of the form

\displaystyle\nu_{0}^{-1}U_{s}\cong B_{\varepsilon}(s)\times B_{3\epsilon}(0)/\Gamma.

We can do this in a way such that for each
0<c
there exists and
0<\varepsilon
such that

\displaystyle\left|\left|g_{0}-g_{S}(s)\oplus g_{0;V;s}\right|\right|_{C^{0,\alpha}}<c

holds on
\mathcal{U}_{\varepsilon}
. As
g_{0}
is uniformally close to the product structures on
\nu_{0}^{-1}\mathcal{U}_{\varepsilon}
, so is the Hermitian Dirac bundle structure. Let
r=r(s,m)
denote the radius of the second component of
B_{\varepsilon}(s)\times\mathbb{R}^{m}/\Gamma
. Take the sets
U_{s}=B_{\varepsilon/2}(s)\times B_{3\epsilon}(0)
and
V_{s}=B_{\varepsilon}(s)\times B_{3\epsilon}(0)
. The local model of the unweighted CF Hölder norm is given by

\displaystyle LHS=

\displaystyle\left|\left|\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{0}(U_{s})}+r^{\alpha}[\Phi]_{C^{0,\alpha}(U_{s})}+\varepsilon\left|\left|\nabla^{H}\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{0}(U_{s})}

\displaystyle+r\left|\left|\nabla^{V}\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{0}(U_{s})}+\epsilon^{\alpha+1}[\nabla^{H}\Phi]_{C^{0,\alpha}(U_{s})}+r^{\alpha+1}[\nabla^{V}\Phi]_{C^{0,\alpha}(U_{s})}

This can be bounded from the above by

\displaystyle LHS\lesssim

\displaystyle\left|\left|\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{0}(U_{s})}+(\varepsilon^{2}+r^{2})^{\alpha/2}[\Phi]_{C^{0,\alpha}(U_{s})}+\varepsilon\left|\left|\nabla^{H}\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{0}(U_{s})}

\displaystyle+r\left|\left|\nabla^{V}\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{0}(U_{s})}+(\varepsilon^{2}+r^{2})^{(\alpha+1)/2}[\nabla\Phi]_{C^{0,\alpha}(U_{s})}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle(\varepsilon^{2}+r^{2})^{1/2}\left|\left|\widehat{D}_{0}\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{0}(V_{s})}+(\varepsilon^{2}+r^{2})^{(\alpha+1)/2}[\widehat{D}_{0}\Phi]_{C^{0,\alpha}(V_{s})}

\displaystyle+\left|\left|\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{0}(V_{s})}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle RHS

Here we used
[
Wal17a
, Prop. 7.7]
and
[
Wal17a
, Prop. 7.11]
where
RHS
is given by

\displaystyle RHS=

\displaystyle r\left|\left|\widehat{D}_{0}\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{0}(V_{s})}+r^{\alpha+1}[\widehat{D}_{0}\Phi]_{C^{0,\alpha}(V_{s})}+\left|\left|\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{0}(V_{s})}

and hence we deduce the local unweighted CF Schauder estimates. By now multiplying with
r^{-\beta}
and as
S
is compact, we can deduce the result.

By Lemma
5.6
the graph norm on
X_{0}\backslash{\mathrm{Tub}}_{i\epsilon}(S)
is uniformally bounded above by

\displaystyle\left|\left|\chi_{i}\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{k+1,\alpha}_{CF;\beta}(\widehat{D}_{0})}\lesssim\left|\left|\chi_{i}\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{k+1,\alpha}_{CF;\beta-1}}.

Let
\nu_{0}^{-1}\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}
denote the cover of
X_{0}\backslash{\mathrm{Tub}}_{i\epsilon}(S)
by opens of the form

\displaystyle\nu_{0}^{-1}U_{s}\cong B_{\varepsilon}(s)\times\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\backslash B_{\epsilon}(0)\right)/\Gamma.

We can do this in a way such that for each
0<c
there exists and
0<\varepsilon
such that

\displaystyle\left|\left|g_{0}-g_{S}(s)\oplus g_{0;V;s}\right|\right|_{C^{0,\alpha}}<c

holds on
\mathcal{U}_{\varepsilon}
. As
g_{0}
is uniformally close to the product structures on
\nu_{0}^{-1}\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}
, so is the Hermitian Dirac bundle structure. Let
r=r(s,m)
denote the radius of the second component of
B_{\varepsilon}(s)\times\mathbb{R}^{m}/\Gamma
. Take the sets
U_{s}=B_{\varepsilon/2}(s)\times\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\backslash B_{1\epsilon}(0)\right)
and
V_{s}=B_{\varepsilon}(s)\times\left(\mathbb{R}^{m}\backslash B_{1\epsilon}(0)\right)
. The local model of the unweighted CF Hölder norm is given by

\displaystyle LHS=

\displaystyle\left|\left|\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{0}(U_{s})}+r^{\alpha}[\Phi]_{C^{0,\alpha}(U_{s})}+\varepsilon\left|\left|\nabla^{H}\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{0}(U_{s})}

\displaystyle+r\left|\left|\nabla^{V}\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{0}(U_{s})}+\epsilon^{\alpha+1}[\nabla^{H}\Phi]_{C^{0,\alpha}(U_{s})}+r^{\alpha+1}[\nabla^{V}\Phi]_{C^{0,\alpha}(U_{s})}

This can be bounded from the above by

\displaystyle LHS\lesssim

\displaystyle\left|\left|\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{0}(U_{s})}+(\varepsilon^{2}+r^{2})^{\alpha/2}[\Phi]_{C^{0,\alpha}(U_{s})}+\varepsilon\left|\left|\nabla^{H}\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{0}(U_{s})}

\displaystyle+r\left|\left|\nabla^{V}\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{0}(U_{s})}+(\varepsilon^{2}+r^{2})^{(\alpha+1)/2}[\nabla\Phi]_{C^{0,\alpha}(U_{s})}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle(\varepsilon^{2}+r^{2})^{1/2}\left|\left|\widehat{D}_{0}\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{0}(V_{s})}+(\varepsilon^{2}+r^{2})^{(\alpha+1)/2}[\widehat{D}_{0}\Phi]_{C^{0,\alpha}(V_{s})}

\displaystyle+\left|\left|\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{0}(V_{s})}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle RHS

Here we used
[
Wal17a
, Prop. 7.7]
and
[
Wal17a
, Prop. 7.11]
where
RHS
is given by

\displaystyle RHS=

\displaystyle\varepsilon\left|\left|\widehat{D}_{0}\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{0}(V_{s})}+\varepsilon^{\alpha+1}[\widehat{D}_{0}\Phi]_{C^{0,\alpha}(V_{s})}+\left|\left|\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{0}(V_{s})}

and hence we deduce the local unweighted CF Schauder estimates. By now multiplying with
r^{1-\beta}
and as
S
is compact, we can deduce the result.

Higher regularity follows directly either from Proposition
4.1
by the higher regularity bounds of the right-inverse of
\widehat{D}_{0}
or by the following commutator estimates.

In order to bootstrap the result to
k>1
, we use that

\displaystyle[\nabla^{\otimes_{0}},\widehat{D}_{0}]=-{\mathrm{cl}}_{g_{0}}([\nabla^{\otimes_{0}},\nabla^{\otimes_{0}}])

\displaystyle[\nabla^{\otimes_{0};H},\widehat{D}_{0}]=

\displaystyle-{\mathrm{cl}}_{g_{0}}([\nabla^{\otimes_{0};H},\nabla^{\otimes_{0};H}])-{\mathrm{cl}}_{g_{0}}([\nabla^{\otimes_{0};H},\nabla^{\otimes_{0};V}])

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-{\mathrm{cl}}_{g_{0}}([\nabla^{\otimes_{0};H},\nabla^{\otimes_{0};H}])

\displaystyle[r\nabla^{\otimes_{0}}_{U},\widehat{D}_{0}]=

\displaystyle-r{\mathrm{cl}}_{g_{0}}([\nabla^{\otimes_{0};V},\nabla^{\otimes_{0};H}])-r{\mathrm{cl}}_{g_{0}}([\nabla^{\otimes_{0};V},\nabla^{\otimes_{0};V}])-D_{0;V}.

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-D_{0;V}

Then

\displaystyle\left|\left|\nabla^{1,0}\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{k,\alpha}_{CF;\beta}}\lesssim

\displaystyle\left|\left|\widehat{D}_{0}\nabla^{1,0}\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{k-1,\alpha}_{CF;\beta-1}}+\left|\left|\nabla^{1,0}\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{0}_{CF;\beta}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\left|\left|\nabla^{1,0}\widehat{D}_{0}\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{k-1,\alpha}_{CF;\beta-1}}+\left|\left|{\mathrm{cl}}_{g_{0}}([\nabla^{\otimes_{0};H},\nabla^{\otimes_{0};H}])\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{k-1,\alpha}_{CF;\beta-1}}+\left|\left|\nabla^{1,0}\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{0}_{CF;\beta}}

and

\displaystyle\left|\left|r\nabla^{0,1}\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{k,\alpha}_{CF;\beta}}\lesssim

\displaystyle\left|\left|\widehat{D}_{0}r\nabla^{0,1}\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{k-1,\alpha}_{CF;\beta-1}}+\left|\left|r\nabla^{0,1}\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{0}_{CF;\beta}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\left|\left|r\nabla^{0,1}\widehat{D}_{0}\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{k-1,\alpha}_{CF;\beta-1}}+\left|\left|D_{0;V}\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{k-1,\alpha}_{CF;\beta-1}}+\left|\left|r\nabla^{0,1}\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{0}_{CF;\beta}}.

Now, as
{\mathrm{supp}}(\Phi)\subset{\mathrm{Tub}}_{3\epsilon}(S)
,
\left|\left|{\mathrm{cl}}_{g_{0}}([\nabla^{\otimes_{0};H},\nabla^{\otimes_{0};H}])\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{k,\alpha}_{CF;\beta}}\lesssim\epsilon\left|\left|\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{k-1,\alpha}_{CF;\beta}}
. Further
D_{0;V}:C^{k+1,\alpha}_{CF;\beta}\rightarrow C^{k,\alpha}_{CF;\beta-1}
is bounded and satisfies the estimate

\displaystyle\left|\left|\Phi\right|\right|_{C^{k+1,\alpha}_{CF;\beta}}
